### Tool result

_source line 11 · 2026-05-17T20:06:42.435Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KFq7MAaM6scvtb99GBAM2m`</summary>

```text
paper/related_work.tex:4:TEAL~\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude, achieving 40--50\% sparsity with no training cost.
paper/related_work.tex:10:ShadowLLM~\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision, providing intermediate coupling within each layer but no end-to-end gradient signal.
paper/experiments.tex:28:At 30\% sparsity, KL distillation with global TEAL allocation achieves PPL 8.98, reducing TEAL's degradation from dense (10.97 $\to$ 8.98, an 18\% improvement). % exp_id: kl_30_global_teal
paper/experiments.tex:34:However, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\pm$0.2\% vs.\ 28.0$\pm$0.6\%, both $\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\%), indicating a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global
paper/experiments.tex:151:Table~\ref{tab:phase} presents global vs.\ uniform allocation at three sparsity levels, revealing qualitative shifts in allocation preference. Denser sampling (e.g., 35\% sparsity) would be needed to precisely locate region boundaries.
paper/experiments.tex:188:PPL remains low (8.36) but downstream accuracy degrades, confirming PPL is a poor proxy for reasoning.
paper/experiments.tex:191:Global/uniform gap vanishes (MMLU 28.1$\pm$0.2\% vs.\ 28.0$\pm$0.6\%, $\sigma\leq$0.6pp; contrast 9.5pp at 30\%), confirming capacity limitation. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform
paper/experiments.tex:201:At 50\% sparsity, the predictor's hidden-state perturbation (L2=482.65) and SPON's additive correction (L2=76.2, cosine similarity 0.196) are weakly correlated ($R^2$=0.038); SPON yields only 2.4\% PPL improvement (12.08$\to$11.79), confirming the KL predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42
paper/experiments.tex:224:The KL advantage ratio is consistent across architectures (within 8\% at $\leq$50\% sparsity; 4.63$\times$ vs.\ 5.59$\times$ at 70\%), providing preliminary evidence of generality at the 7--8B scale.
paper/experiments.tex:227:On Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25} (dense PPL 5.18), KL achieves PPL 7.62 at 30\% ($1.47\times$ dense, matching the 8B ratio); BCE collapses to 6.4\% native sparsity ($10\times$ stronger penalty also fails), confirming architecture-general gradient insufficiency (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}). % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42_v2
paper/experiments.tex:232:The pipeline adds 6.4\% overhead to dense inference while removing the compensation network ($-$2.7\% latency, $-$67.2M params); post-hoc SPON yields $<$3\% additional gain (\S\ref{sec:spon}), confirming the predictor internalizes most error-correction signal (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}).
paper/conclusion.tex:4:We showed that KL distillation resolves the structural mismatch between per-neuron BCE training and global top-$k$ inference allocation, enabling a lightweight predictor ($<$1\% of base) to halve TEAL's perplexity at 50\% sparsity on both LLaMA-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B-v0.3.
paper/conclusion.tex:5:Uniform per-layer budgets reduce cross-seed downstream standard deviation by $7.5\times$ at 30\% sparsity, providing predictable guarantees; future work includes scaling beyond 7--8B and downstream-aware training objectives.
paper/method.tex:5:The method replaces per-neuron binary classification (BCE) with an output-distribution objective that couples all pruning decisions through the model's forward pass, enabling competitive performance without auxiliary error compensation networks at inference.
paper/method.tex:58:At high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons, enabling broad exploration.
paper/method.tex:139:\item[\textbf{(d)}] The Gumbel-Sigmoid derivative, providing the differentiable path from logit to mask.
paper/limitations.tex:8:At 30\% sparsity, global allocation exhibits 9.5pp cross-seed MMLU standard deviation (3-seed), indicating that allocation strategy interacts with predictor initialization in task-specific ways; this variance is MMLU-specific, as ARC, WinoGrande, and GSM8K show stable performance across seeds under both allocation strategies.
paper/abstract.tex:3:Gradient analysis reveals a structural mismatch: BCE's per-neuron gradient is blind to cross-neuron interactions, achieving higher oracle F1 yet 137\% worse perplexity than a KL-trained predictor.
paper/abstract.tex:6:At higher sparsity (50\%), PPL advantage widens (49\% over TEAL) but downstream tasks degrade regardless of allocation strategy, revealing allocation-dependent operating regions at the 7--8B scale.
paper/appendix.tex:29:Eliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\sim$2.7\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling), confirming that KL distillation provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.
paper/appendix.tex:135:The 40\% target converges to $\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\% deviation, illustrating the controller's precision when the target is reachable without saturating.
paper/appendix.tex:145:A mild loss increase in the final $\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\tau \to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, confirming the training is converging toward the hard-mask inference regime.
paper/appendix.tex:171:The KL-trained predictor achieves 52.5\% relative advantage over TEAL on out-of-domain data (18.40 vs.\ 38.75), comparable to or slightly exceeding the 49\% advantage observed on WikiText-2 (11.79 vs.\ 23.31), suggesting that KL-trained predictions generalize to held-out data when the evaluation distribution differs from training.
paper/appendix.tex:189:Three-seed evaluation ($s \in \{42, 123, 456\}$) at 50\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\%), confirming training stability across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform
paper/appendix.tex:192:Under global allocation at 50\%, MMLU is equally stable: $28.1 \pm 0.2$\% (s42=27.89, s123=28.34, s456=27.94), confirming that the near-random performance is capacity-limited rather than seed-sensitive ($\sigma$=0.2pp vs.\ 9.5pp at 30\% global). % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global
paper/introduction.tex:24:\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity, revealing that oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled: BCE achieves +7pp F1 yet 137\% worse PPL (\S\ref{sec:isocompute}).
```

vs.\\ 23.31), suggesting that KL-trained predictions generalize to held-out data when the evaluation distribution differs from training.\npaper/appendix.tex:189:Three-seed evaluation ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$) at 50\\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \\pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\\%), confirming training stability across random initializations. % exp_id: _source line 21 · 2026-05-17T20:07:05.720Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KDd4eCAXqkc4HzW3Pua2Ft`</summary>

```text
1	We study whether end-to-end KL distillation can replace per-neuron classification for activation sparsity prediction in SwiGLU LLMs.
2	Existing predictors trained with binary cross-entropy (BCE) optimize each neuron independently, but inference allocates sparsity budgets via global top-$k$ across all layers.
3	Gradient analysis reveals a structural mismatch: BCE's per-neuron gradient is blind to cross-neuron interactions, achieving higher oracle F1 yet 137\% worse perplexity than a KL-trained predictor.
4	A lightweight predictor ($<$1\% of base parameters) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation resolves this mismatch by coupling all neurons through the output distribution.
5	At the recommended 30\% sparsity operating point on LLaMA-3.1-8B, KL distillation reduces PPL degradation by 18\% over magnitude-based TEAL; uniform allocation provides $7.5\times$ smaller cross-seed downstream standard deviation than global top-$k$, eliminating seed-dependent performance instability.
6	At higher sparsity (50\%), PPL advantage widens (49\% over TEAL) but downstream tasks degrade regardless of allocation strategy, revealing allocation-dependent operating regions at the 7--8B scale.
7	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 confirms consistent improvements (within 8\% ratio at $\leq$50\% sparsity).
8	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018CFxjpg2g8BkLW3XpSFagq`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	SwiGLU feed-forward networks~\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.
4	Learned sparsity predictors~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.
5	Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: TEAL's top-$k$ mechanism~\cite{liu2025teal} ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.
6	This mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.
7	
8	We provide this analysis.
9	The gradient of BCE with respect to neuron $j$'s score is $\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} / \partial s_j = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$, a function of $j$ alone, independent of every other neuron.
10	Under KL divergence, the gradient for neuron $j$ in layer $\ell$ factors as:
11	\begin{equation*}
12	\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}}}{\partial s_j^{(\ell)}} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}}}{\partial \mathbf{z}} \cdot \frac{\partial \mathbf{z}}{\partial \mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}} \cdot \frac{\partial \mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}}{\partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}} \cdot \frac{\partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}}{\partial s_j^{(\ell)}},
13	\end{equation*}
14	where the Jacobian $\partial \mathbf{z} / \partial \mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}$ propagates through all downstream layers, coupling each neuron's gradient to every other neuron's mask state.
15	This structural difference predicts that KL should outperform BCE specifically under global allocation, and the prediction is confirmed empirically: BCE achieves higher oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\ 0.540) yet produces 137\% worse perplexity, because per-neuron magnitude accuracy is a misaligned proxy for output-distribution preservation.
16	
17	To test whether end-to-end distillation can overcome this mismatch, we instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.
18	With uniform allocation, the predictor achieves PPL 11.96 at 50\% sparsity; under global top-$k$, PPL improves to 11.79 (49\% over TEAL) but late-layer starvation amplifies cross-seed downstream standard deviation ($7.5\times$), motivating uniform allocation for deployment.
19	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral} confirms consistent improvements at the 7--8B scale (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy} situates prior work along correction granularity and training signal coupling).
20	
21	Our contributions are:
22	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	\item Quantitative characterization of the structural mismatch between per-neuron BCE training and global top-$k$ inference allocation, supported by oracle F1 decomposition and gradient SNR measurements (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}).
24	\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity, revealing that oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled: BCE achieves +7pp F1 yet 137\% worse PPL (\S\ref{sec:isocompute}).
25	\item Analysis of how allocation strategy governs the prediction-to-downstream gap, identifying a late-layer starvation mechanism and characterizing qualitative shifts across sparsity levels at the 7--8B scale (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
26	\end{itemize}
27	
```

(Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy} situates prior work along correction granularity and training signal coupling).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item Quantitative characterization of the structural mismatch between per-neuron BCE training and global top-$k$ inference allocation, supported by oracle F1 decomposition and gradient SNR measurements (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\n\\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity, revealing that oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled: BCE achieves +7pp F1 yet 137\\% worse PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Analysis of how allocation strategy governs the prediction-to-downstream gap, identifying a late-layer starvation mechanism and characterizing qualitative shifts across sparsity levels at the 7--8B scale (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 27,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 27
    }

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	
6	\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}
7	We evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.
8	The sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Training.}
11	All predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4, using AdamW~\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $5 \times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.
12	The KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.
13	
14	\paragraph{Evaluation.}
15	We report perplexity on WikiText-2~\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), and GSM8K (8-shot).
16	
17	\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}
18	At inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.
19	We term this \textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.
20	
21	\paragraph{Baselines.}
22	We compare against: (1) \textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly; (2) \textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; (3) \textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers; and (4) \textbf{WINA}~\cite{liu2025teal}, an importance-weighted variant.
23	
24	
25	\subsection{Main Results}
26	
27	Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. We recommend uniform allocation for deployment (best downstream preservation); global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
28	At 30\% sparsity, KL distillation with global TEAL allocation achieves PPL 8.98, reducing TEAL's degradation from dense (10.97 $\to$ 8.98, an 18\% improvement). % exp_id: kl_30_global_teal
29	The advantage holds across additional downstream benchmarks (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}).
30	Uniform allocation at 30\% provides stable downstream performance (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed) while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\pm$9.5\%), with late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\%) interacting with predictor initialization (\S\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_3seed
31	
32	At 50\% sparsity, KL distillation over vanilla TEAL widens the PPL advantage: 11.79 vs.\ 23.31, a 49\% reduction. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bench_teal50
33	The predictor also generalizes to held-out data (C4 validation: 52.5\% advantage over TEAL; see Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
34	However, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\pm$0.2\% vs.\ 28.0$\pm$0.6\%, both $\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\%), indicating a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global
35	
36	\begin{table}[t]
37	\centering
38	\caption{Main results on LLaMA-3.1-8B. PPL$\downarrow$ on WikiText-2; accuracy$\uparrow$ on downstream tasks. \textbf{Bold}: best among compensation-free methods per sparsity level. BCE follows the DejaVu~\cite{liu2023dejavu} predictor pipeline (per-neuron BCE on magnitude oracles) with a FastForward-style~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} compensation network (67.2M params) applied post-masking; no TEAL allocation. KL MMLU at 30\% and 50\% reports 3-seed mean$\pm$std; 50\% uniform PPL is 3-seed ($11.92 \pm 0.07$; Appendix~\ref{app:stability}); all other entries use seed 42.}
39	\label{tab:main}
40	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
41	\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
42	\toprule
43	Sparsity & Method & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\
44	\midrule
45	0\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\
46	\midrule
47	\multirow{3}{*}{30\%}
48	& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & \textbf{42.32} & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\ % exp_id: bench_teal30
49	& KL global (Ours) & 8.98 & 39.68 & \textbf{70.17} & 35.7$\pm$9.5 & \textbf{8.79} \\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global
50	& KL uniform & \textbf{8.82} & 40.78 & 68.59 & \textbf{40.7$\pm$1.3} & 7.35 \\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_uniform
51	\midrule
52	\multirow{4}{*}{50\%}
53	& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\ % exp_id: bench_teal50
54	& KL global (Ours) & \textbf{11.79} & \textbf{32.85} & \textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global
55	& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\pm$0.6 & \textbf{2.12} \\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform
56	& BCE (2700 steps)$^\ast$ & 27.97 & 23.98 & 50.59 & 30.02 & 4.85 \\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42
57	\bottomrule
58	\end{tabular}}
59	\end{table}
60	
61	
62	\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}
63	\label{sec:isocompute}
64	
65	To test whether BCE simply requires more compute, we train it for 2700 steps (2.71$\times$ the KL step budget, approximately equal wall-clock time: $\sim$46 min BCE vs.\ $\sim$45 min KL on RTX PRO 6000).
66	The BCE predictor achieves PPL 27.97, still 2.37$\times$ worse than KL at 1000 steps (PPL 11.79). % exp_id: bce_isocompute_s42
67	The failure is structural, not due to architectural or compute differences: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\ 0.540) with 137\% worse PPL (27.97 vs.\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) confirms it learns a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude matching. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42
68	Future work could further isolate coupling effects through MSE on hidden states and per-layer KL ablations.
69	
70	
71	\subsection{Ablation Studies}
72	
73	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 50\% sparsity.
74	
75	\begin{table}[t]
76	\centering
77	\caption{Ablation at 50\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$, unified benchmark protocol).}
78	\label{tab:ablation}
79	\small
80	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
81	\toprule
82	Configuration & PPL$\downarrow$ & $\Delta$ from Ours \\
83	\midrule
84	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{KL-based variants}} \\
85	Forward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \textbf{11.79} & --- \\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global
86	JSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 12.54 & +0.75 \\ % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42
87	Reverse KL + Gumbel & 12.80 & +1.01 \\ % exp_id: reverse_kl_ablation_s42
88	Forward KL + STE & 15.77 & +3.98 \\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42
89	\midrule
90	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\
91	BCE + Compensation (hard mask) & 25.44 & +13.65 \\ % exp_id: bce_comp_staged_s42
92	BCE (no compensation) & 27.97 & +16.18 \\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42
93	BCE + Gumbel + Penalty (no KL) & 38.02 & +26.23 \\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation
94	\bottomrule
95	\end{tabular}
96	\end{table}
97	
98	\paragraph{Gumbel vs.\ STE.}
99	Replacing Gumbel-Sigmoid with STE~\cite{bengio2013ste} degrades PPL from 11.79 to 15.77 (+3.98), accounting for 34.5\% of the improvement over vanilla TEAL. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42
100	
101	\paragraph{Divergence Direction.}
102	Forward KL (mode-covering) outperforms JSD (+6.4\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\%), consistent with the SNR analysis in \S\ref{sec:method:gradient}: complete teacher supervision enables better global allocation learning. % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42
103	This contrasts with MiniLLM~\cite{gu2024minillm}, which finds reverse KL superior for sequence-level distillation: in generation, mode-seeking prevents low-probability token production, while in mask prediction, mode-covering (forward KL) ensures no important neuron is omitted ($q(x) \approx 0$ where $p(x) > 0$ is directly penalized).
104	
105	\paragraph{SPON Contribution.}
106	Adding SPON~\cite{xu2026spon} bias correction reduces PPL from 12.08 to 11.79 (2.4\% relative); see \S\ref{sec:spon} for decomposition.\footnote{SPON analysis uses \texttt{evaluate.py}; main results use the unified benchmark.} % exp_id: spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42
107	
108	\paragraph{Loss Function Isolation.}
109	BCE with identical Gumbel-Sigmoid and penalty yields PPL 38.02, worse than vanilla BCE (27.97): the penalty interferes with per-neuron optimization without global coordination. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation
110	The loss effect ($\Delta$PPL=26.23) is $6.6\times$ the relaxation effect ($\Delta$PPL=3.98).
111	
112	\paragraph{BCE + Compensation.}
113	FastForward-style compensation~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} improves BCE from 27.97 to 25.44 (9.0\%), still $2.16\times$ worse than KL. % exp_id: bce_comp_staged_s42
114	
115	\paragraph{Hyperparameter Robustness.}
116	Appendix~\ref{app:db_sensitivity} confirms robustness to predictor capacity ($d_b$, $<$3\%), temperature ($<$1.8\%), and training steps (saturated by 800). % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep
117	
118	
119	\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}
120	\label{sec:scaling}
121	
122	Table~\ref{tab:scaling} and Figure~\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep} show PPL as a function of sparsity for the KL predictor under global allocation and vanilla TEAL.
123	
124	\begin{figure}[t]
125	\centering
126	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}
127	\caption{Perplexity scaling with sparsity. The KL predictor's advantage grows disproportionately as more neurons are pruned.}
128	\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}
129	\end{figure}
130	
131	\begin{table}[t]
132	\centering
133	\caption{PPL scaling with sparsity (WikiText-2). KL advantage grows disproportionately with sparsity.}
134	\label{tab:scaling}
135	\small
136	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
137	\toprule
138	Sparsity & KL (Ours) & TEAL vanilla & Relative advantage \\
139	\midrule
140	30\% & \textbf{8.98} & 10.97 & 18\% \\ % exp_id: kl_30_global_teal
141	50\% & \textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 49\% \\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global
142	70\% & \textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 82\% \\ % exp_id: kl_70_global_teal
143	\bottomrule
144	\end{tabular}
145	\end{table}
146	
147	The KL advantage grows from 18\% at 30\% to 82\% at 70\%, where TEAL collapses (PPL 100.11) while the learned predictor maintains coherent allocation.
148	In absolute terms, KL/Dense degradation is $1.47\times$ at 30\%, $1.93\times$ at 50\%, and $2.93\times$ at 70\%, compared to TEAL/Dense ratios of $1.79\times$, $3.81\times$, and $16.4\times$ respectively.
149	
150	\paragraph{Three Operating Regions.}
151	Table~\ref{tab:phase} presents global vs.\ uniform allocation at three sparsity levels, revealing qualitative shifts in allocation preference. Denser sampling (e.g., 35\% sparsity) would be needed to precisely locate region boundaries.
152	
153	\begin{table}[t]
154	\centering
155	\caption{Allocation analysis: global (TEAL) vs.\ uniform per-layer allocation across sparsity levels. 30\% and 50\% KL rows report 3-seed mean$\pm$std for MMLU; other metrics use seed 42. Each row uses a predictor trained at the respective sparsity target.}
156	\label{tab:phase}
157	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
158	\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
159	\toprule
160	Sparsity & Allocation & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\
161	\midrule
162	0\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\
163	\midrule
164	\multirow{3}{*}{30\%}
165	& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & \textbf{42.32} & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\ % exp_id: bench_teal30
166	& KL global & 8.98 & 39.68 & \textbf{70.17} & 35.7$\pm$9.5 & \textbf{8.79} \\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global
167	& KL uniform & \textbf{8.82} & 40.78 & 68.59 & \textbf{40.7$\pm$1.3} & 7.35 \\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_uniform
168	\midrule
169	\multirow{2}{*}{40\%}
170	& KL global & 10.12 & 40.96 & 65.35 & \textbf{38.06} & \textbf{5.00} \\ % exp_id: bench_kl40_global
171	& KL uniform & \textbf{8.36} & \textbf{41.81} & \textbf{67.17} & 30.68 & 3.94 \\ % exp_id: d030_kl40_uniform
172	\midrule
173	\multirow{3}{*}{50\%}
174	& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\ % exp_id: bench_teal50
175	& KL global & \textbf{11.79} & \textbf{32.85} & \textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global
176	& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\pm$0.6 & \textbf{2.12} \\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform
177	\bottomrule

179	\end{table}
180	
181	The allocation preference is \emph{non-monotonic}. We observe three qualitative behaviors at the sampled sparsity levels (7--8B scale):
182	
183	\noindent\textbf{Budget-surplus ($\leq$30\%).}
184	Uniform allocation provides $7.5\times$ smaller cross-seed MMLU standard deviation ($\sigma$: 1.3pp vs.\ 9.5pp), eliminating catastrophic seed-dependent degradation under global top-$k$ (MMLU 35.7$\pm$9.5\% vs.\ 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed). Global top-$k$ starves late layers (L31: 28.4\%, std/mean$=$1.89), and this starvation interacts unpredictably with predictor initialization (Appendix~\ref{app:lagrangian}). % exp_id: kl30_3seed
185	
186	\noindent\textbf{Budget-constrained ($\sim$40\%).}
187	The preference reverses: global achieves MMLU 38.06\% vs.\ uniform's 30.68\% (+7.4pp). % exp_id: bench_kl40_global, d030_kl40_uniform
188	PPL remains low (8.36) but downstream accuracy degrades, confirming PPL is a poor proxy for reasoning.
189	
190	\noindent\textbf{Capacity-limited ($\geq$50\%).}
191	Global/uniform gap vanishes (MMLU 28.1$\pm$0.2\% vs.\ 28.0$\pm$0.6\%, $\sigma\leq$0.6pp; contrast 9.5pp at 30\%), confirming capacity limitation. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform
192	Late-layer starvation persists across levels (L24--31 retention: 50.1/34.2/22.6\% at 30/50/70\% sparsity; Figure~\ref{fig:retention}), consistent on both 7--8B models (\S\ref{sec:cross_arch}).
193	
194	\paragraph{Gap Decomposition.}
195	Under uniform allocation, 53\% of the Dense$\to$KL gap is predictor approximation, 47\% budget allocation (Appendix~\ref{app:db_sensitivity}); under global, KL remains $1.98\times$ better than TEAL.
196	
197	
198	\subsection{SPON Orthogonality Analysis}
199	\label{sec:spon}
200	
201	At 50\% sparsity, the predictor's hidden-state perturbation (L2=482.65) and SPON's additive correction (L2=76.2, cosine similarity 0.196) are weakly correlated ($R^2$=0.038); SPON yields only 2.4\% PPL improvement (12.08$\to$11.79), confirming the KL predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42
202	
203	\paragraph{Cross-Architecture Validation.}
204	\label{sec:cross_arch}
205	Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch} evaluates on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral} (GQA + sliding window, dense PPL 5.52).
206	
207	\begin{table}[t]
208	\centering
209	\caption{Cross-architecture comparison (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$). Adv = TEAL\,/\,KL ratio. LLaMA uses global top-$k$; Mistral uses uniform allocation (matched within each architecture).}
210	\label{tab:cross_arch}

212	\begin{tabular}{l@{\hspace{4pt}}cc@{\hspace{3pt}}c@{\hspace{6pt}}|@{\hspace{6pt}}cc@{\hspace{3pt}}c}
213	\toprule
214	 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{Mistral-7B} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{LLaMA-8B} \\
215	 & KL & TEAL & Adv & KL & TEAL & Adv \\
216	\midrule
217	30\% & \textbf{6.77} & 8.38 & 1.24$\times$ & \textbf{8.98} & 10.97 & 1.22$\times$ \\ % exp_id: kl_30_mistral, teal_30_mistral
218	50\% & \textbf{8.65} & 18.31 & 2.12$\times$ & \textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 1.98$\times$ \\ % exp_id: kl_50_mistral, teal_50_mistral
219	70\% & \textbf{13.33} & 61.72 & 4.63$\times$ & \textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 5.59$\times$ \\ % exp_id: kl_70_mistral, teal_70_mistral
220	\bottomrule
221	\end{tabular}
222	\end{table}
223	
224	The KL advantage ratio is consistent across architectures (within 8\% at $\leq$50\% sparsity; 4.63$\times$ vs.\ 5.59$\times$ at 70\%), providing preliminary evidence of generality at the 7--8B scale.
225	
226	\paragraph{Scale Generalization: Qwen-2.5-14B.}
227	On Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25} (dense PPL 5.18), KL achieves PPL 7.62 at 30\% ($1.47\times$ dense, matching the 8B ratio); BCE collapses to 6.4\% native sparsity ($10\times$ stronger penalty also fails), confirming architecture-general gradient insufficiency (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}). % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42_v2
228	% TODO: add vanilla TEAL 14B PPL when qwen14b_teal30_eval completes
229	
230	
231	\paragraph{Inference Efficiency.}
232	The pipeline adds 6.4\% overhead to dense inference while removing the compensation network ($-$2.7\% latency, $-$67.2M params); post-hoc SPON yields $<$3\% additional gain (\S\ref{sec:spon}), confirming the predictor internalizes most error-correction signal (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}).
233	
234	\paragraph{Training Stability.}
235	Three-seed evaluation (50\%): PPL $11.92 \pm 0.07$, MMLU $\sigma\leq$0.6pp (Appendix~\ref{app:stability}).
236

We recommend uniform allocation for deployment (best downstream preservation); global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nAt 30\\% sparsity, KL distillation with global TEAL allocation achieves PPL 8.98, reducing TEAL's degradation from dense (10.97 $\\to$ 8.98, an 18\\% improvement). % exp_id: kl_30_global_teal\nThe advantage holds across additional downstream benchmarks (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\nUniform allocation at 30\\% provides stable downstream performance (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed) while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\\pm$9.5\\%), with late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\\%) interacting with predictor initialization (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_3seed\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, KL distillation over vanilla TEAL widens the PPL advantage: 11.79 vs.\\ 23.31, a 49\\% reduction. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bench_teal50\nThe predictor also generalizes to held-out data (C4 validation: 52.5\\% advantage over TEAL; see Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nHowever, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\\pm$0.2\\% vs.\\ 28.0$\\pm$0.6\\%, both $\\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\\%), indicating a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results on LLaMA-3.1-8B. PPL$\\downarrow$ on WikiText-2; accuracy$\\uparrow$ on downstream tasks. \\textbf{Bold}: best among compensation-free methods per sparsity level. BCE follows the DejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} predictor pipeline (per-neuron BCE on magnitude oracles) with a FastForward-style~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} compensation network (67.2M params) applied post-masking; no TEAL allocation. KL MMLU at 30\\% and 50\\% reports 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; 50\\% uniform PPL is 3-seed ($11.92 \\pm 0.07$; Appendix~\\ref{app:stability}); all other entries use seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:main}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & \\textbf{42.32} & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30\n& KL global (Ours) & 8.98 & 39.68 & \\textbf{70.17} & 35.7$\\pm$9.5 & \\textbf{8.79} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.82} & 40.78 & 68.59 & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} & 7.35 \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{50\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \\textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50\n& KL global (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{32.85} & \\textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.12} \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n& BCE (2700 steps)$^\\ast$ & 27.97 & 23.98 & 50.59 & 30.02 & 4.85 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}\n\\label{sec:isocompute}\n\nTo test whether BCE simply requires more compute, we train it for 2700 steps (2.71$\\times$ the KL step budget, approximately equal wall-clock time: $\\sim$46 min BCE vs.\\ $\\sim$45 min KL on RTX PRO 6000).\nThe BCE predictor achieves PPL 27.97, still 2.37$\\times$ worse than KL at 1000 steps (PPL 11.79). % exp_id: bce_isocompute_s42\nThe failure is structural, not due to architectural or compute differences: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\\ 0.540) with 137\\% worse PPL (27.97 vs.\\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) confirms it learns a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude matching. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42\nFuture work could further isolate coupling effects through MSE on hidden states and per-layer KL ablations.\n\n\n\\subsection{Ablation Studies}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design \\\\ % exp_id: reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nForward KL + STE & 15.77 & +3.98 \\\\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\\\\nBCE + Compensation (hard mask) & 25.44 & +13.65 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_comp_staged_s42\nBCE (no compensation) & 27.97 & +16.18 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42\nBCE + Gumbel + Penalty (no KL) & 38.02 & +26.23 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Gumbel vs.\\ STE.}\nReplacing Gumbel-Sigmoid with STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} degrades PPL from 11.79 to 15.77 (+3.98), accounting for 34.5\\% of the improvement over vanilla TEAL. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Divergence Direction.}\nForward KL (mode-covering) outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with the SNR analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}: complete teacher supervision enables better global allocation learning. % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nThis contrasts with MiniLLM~\\cite{gu2024minillm}, which finds reverse KL superior for sequence-level distillation: in generation, mode-seeking prevents low-probability token production, while in mask prediction, mode-covering (forward KL) ensures no important neuron is omitted ($q(x) \\approx 0$ where $p(x) > 0$ is directly penalized).\n\n\\paragraph{SPON 1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We present a KL distillation framework for training contextual sparsity predictors in SwiGLU-based language models.
5	The method replaces per-neuron binary classification (BCE) with an output-distribution objective that couples all pruning decisions through the model's forward pass, enabling competitive performance without auxiliary error compensation networks at inference.
6	
7	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
8	\label{sec:method:formulation}
9	
10	Consider a pretrained transformer with $L$ layers, each containing a SwiGLU feed-forward network~\cite{shazeer2020glu}.
11	For layer $\ell$, the FFN computes:
12	\begin{align}
13	\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)} = \bigl(\sigma(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_g^{(\ell)}) \odot \mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}\bigr) \mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)},
14	\label{eq:swiglu}
15	\end{align}
16	where $\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention ($d{=}4096$), $\sigma$ denotes SiLU, $\mathbf{W}_g^{(\ell)}, \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d_{\text{ff}}}$ and $\mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{ff}} \times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.
17	
18	Define the gate activation $\mathbf{g}^{(\ell)} = \sigma(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_g^{(\ell)}) \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{ff}}}$.
19	Each element $g_j^{(\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.
20	We introduce a binary mask $\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)} \in \{0,1\}^{d_{\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:
21	\begin{align}
22	\mathbf{y}_{\text{sparse}}^{(\ell)} = \bigl(\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{g}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}\bigr) \mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)}.
23	\label{eq:sparse_ffn}
24	\end{align}
25	Our goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\theta^{(\ell)}(\mathbf{h}) \to \mathbf{s}^{(\ell)} \in (0,1)^{d_{\text{ff}}}$ that produces per-neuron importance scores from the pre-FFN hidden state $\mathbf{h}$.
26	At inference, the scores are fed to a global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\cite{liu2025teal} that distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly, retaining the $(1{-}\rho) \times L \times d_{\text{ff}}$ highest-scoring neurons model-wide for a target sparsity ratio $\rho$.
27	
28	
29	\subsection{Predictor Architecture}
30	\label{sec:method:predictor}
31	
32	Each layer $\ell$ has an independent two-layer MLP predictor:
33	\begin{align}
34	f_\theta^{(\ell)}(\mathbf{h}) = \text{GELU}(\mathbf{h} \mathbf{W}_1^{(\ell)}) \, \mathbf{W}_2^{(\ell)},
35	\label{eq:predictor}
36	\end{align}
37	where $\mathbf{W}_1^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d_b}$ and $\mathbf{W}_2^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_b \times d_{\text{ff}}}$ with bottleneck dimension $d_b{=}128$.
38	The predictor takes the hidden state after attention (before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\mathbf{s}^{(\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.
39	
40	Each predictor has $(4096 \times 128) + (128 \times 14336) = 2.37\text{M}$ parameters.
41	With 32 independent predictors (one per layer), the total overhead is 75.9M parameters, less than 1\% of the 8B base model.
42	The predictors operate independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.
43	
44	
45	\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}
46	\label{sec:method:gumbel}
47	
48	The hard mask $m_j = \mathbf{1}[s_j > \text{threshold}]$ is non-differentiable, preventing gradient flow from the KL objective through the mask to the predictor parameters.
49	We apply Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} to produce differentiable soft masks:
50	\begin{align}
51	\tilde{m}_j = \sigma_\tau\!\left(\log \frac{s_j}{1 - s_j} + g_1 - g_2\right), \quad g_1, g_2 \sim \text{Gumbel}(0,1),
52	\label{eq:gumbel}
53	\end{align}
54	where $\sigma_\tau(x) = \text{sigmoid}(x / \tau)$ and $\tau$ is a temperature parameter.
55	The Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\tau \to 0$, $\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.
56	
57	We anneal $\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.
58	At high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons, enabling broad exploration.
59	As temperature decreases, the mask concentrates on confident decisions, approximating the hard allocation used at inference.
60	
61	\paragraph{Comparison with Straight-Through Estimation.}
62	The straight-through estimator (STE)~\cite{bengio2013ste} applies a hard threshold in the forward pass and passes gradients through unchanged ($\partial m / \partial s = 1$).
63	In our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously, causing violent oscillations in the penalty weight and failure to converge to the target sparsity.
64	Gumbel relaxation eliminates this pathology, contributing a 34.5\% improvement (PPL 15.77 $\to$ 11.79 at 50\% sparsity), as we verify empirically in \S\ref{sec:experiments}.
65	
66	
67	\subsection{KL Divergence Training Objective}
68	\label{sec:method:kl}
69	
70	Let $\mathbf{z}_{\text{dense}}$ denote the output logits from a full dense forward pass, and $\mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}}$ the logits from a forward pass with predictor-generated soft masks $\{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(\ell)}\}_\ell$ applied at every layer.
71	We minimize the forward KL divergence between the dense and sparse output distributions:
72	\begin{align}
73	\mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} = D_{\text{KL}}(p_{\text{dense}} \,\|\, p_{\text{sparse}}) = \sum_{v} p_{\text{dense}}^{(v)} \log \frac{p_{\text{dense}}^{(v)}}{p_{\text{sparse}}^{(v)}},
74	\label{eq:kl_loss}
75	\end{align}
76	where $p_{\text{dense}} = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{z}_{\text{dense}})$ and $p_{\text{sparse}} = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}})$, summed over the vocabulary $v$ and averaged over sequence positions.
77	The base model is frozen; only the predictor parameters receive gradients.
78	
79	We use forward KL (mean-seeking) rather than reverse KL, as the goal is to preserve the dense model's primary predictions rather than explore alternative modes; \S\ref{sec:experiments} validates this choice with ablations over divergence direction (forward KL outperforms JSD by 6.4\% and reverse KL by 8.6\%).
80	No temperature scaling ($T{=}1$) is applied~\cite{hinton2015distilling}.
81	
82	
83	\subsection{Adaptive Penalty Sparsity Controller}
84	\label{sec:method:lagrangian}
85	
86	We enforce a model-level sparsity target $\rho$ via constrained optimization.
87	Let $\bar{s} = \frac{1}{L} \sum_\ell \frac{1}{d_{\text{ff}}} \sum_j \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}$ denote the average fraction of retained neurons.
88	The constrained problem is:
89	\begin{align}
90	\min_\theta \; \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \bar{s} \leq 1 - \rho.
91	\label{eq:constrained}
92	\end{align}
93	We relax this via an adaptive penalty controller:
94	\begin{align}
95	\mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} + \lambda \cdot \text{clamp}(|\bar{s} - (1 - \rho)| - \epsilon, \min{=}0)^2,
96	\label{eq:lagrangian}
97	\end{align}
98	where $\epsilon = 0.02$ is a deadzone margin that prevents $\lambda$ from responding to small constraint violations.
99	The penalty weight $\lambda$ is updated multiplicatively at each step:
100	\begin{align}
101	\lambda \leftarrow \begin{cases} \lambda \times 2 & \text{if } \bar{s} > (1-\rho) + \epsilon \\ \lambda \times 0.5 & \text{if } \bar{s} < (1-\rho) - \epsilon \\ \lambda & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}
102	\label{eq:dual_update}
103	\end{align}
104	with $\lambda$ clamped to $[\lambda_{\text{floor}}, \lambda_{\max}]$ where $\lambda_{\text{floor}} = 0.01$ and $\lambda_{\max} \in \{1000, 5000\}$ depending on the target.
105	The geometric doubling requires only $\lambda_{\max}$ as a user-specified hyperparameter (no step-size tuning) and reaches target sparsity within 40--110 steps for 40--50\% targets; for 30\% and 70\% targets, $\lambda$ saturates at $\lambda_{\max}$ with 1.7--5.0\% residual deviation (Appendix~\ref{app:lagrangian}, Figure~\ref{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}). The deadzone prevents oscillation once sparsity is within $\pm$2\%.\footnote{Our update rule resembles the augmented Lagrangian method but replaces gradient-based dual updates with a multiplicative schedule for training stability. We term it an adaptive penalty controller to distinguish from formal dual methods that guarantee KKT satisfaction.}
106	
107	
108	\subsection{Gradient-Based Motivation: BCE vs.\ KL}
109	\label{sec:method:gradient}
110	
111	We analyze the structural difference between BCE and KL training signals to motivate why KL distillation is better suited to global top-$k$ inference.
112	
113	\paragraph{BCE gradient structure.}
114	The standard approach trains the predictor against oracle masks $m_j^* = \mathbf{1}[|g_j| \geq \text{top-}k\text{ threshold}]$ derived from the dense model's activation magnitudes, where the threshold is the global top-$k$ cutoff across all layers (matching TEAL's inference protocol).
115	The loss decomposes per neuron:
116	\begin{align}
117	\mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} = -\frac{1}{d_{\text{ff}}} \sum_{j=1}^{d_{\text{ff}}} \bigl[ m_j^* \log \sigma(s_j) + (1 - m_j^*) \log(1 - \sigma(s_j)) \bigr].
118	\label{eq:bce}
119	\end{align}
120	The gradient with respect to predictor logit $s_j$ is:
121	\begin{align}
122	\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}}}{\partial s_j} = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*.
123	\label{eq:bce_grad}
124	\end{align}
125	This gradient depends \emph{only} on neuron $j$'s own prediction and target.
126	Each neuron is trained independently; there is no inter-neuron coupling in the learning signal.
127	
128	\paragraph{KL gradient structure.}
129	Under KL distillation, the gradient for predictor logit $s_j^{(\ell)}$ in layer $\ell$ is:
130	\begin{align}
131	\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}}}{\partial s_j^{(\ell)}} = \underbrace{\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}}}{\partial \mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}}}}_{\text{(a)}} \cdot \underbrace{\frac{\partial \mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}}}{\partial \mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}}}_{\text{(b)}} \cdot \underbrace{\frac{\partial \mathbf{h}^{(\ell)}}{\partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}}}_{\text{(c)}} \cdot \underbrace{\frac{\partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}}{\partial s_j^{(\ell)}}}_{\text{(d)}},
132	\label{eq:kl_grad}
133	\end{align}
134	where each factor has a distinct role:
135	\begin{itemize}
136	\item[\textbf{(a)}] The KL gradient at the output, reflecting how each logit component deviates from the dense distribution.
137	\item[\textbf{(b)}] The Jacobian through all downstream layers from layer $\ell$'s output to the final logits. This factor couples neuron $j$'s gradient to the state of every other retained neuron in layers $\ell, \ell{+}1, \ldots, L$, because the sparse hidden state propagates through subsequent attention and FFN operations.
138	\item[\textbf{(c)}] The local mask effect: $\partial \mathbf{h}^{(\ell)} / \partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)} = (g_j^{(\ell)} \cdot [\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}]_j) \cdot \mathbf{W}_{d,j}^{(\ell)}$, projecting through the down-projection to affect the full $d$-dimensional hidden state.
139	\item[\textbf{(d)}] The Gumbel-Sigmoid derivative, providing the differentiable path from logit to mask.
140	\end{itemize}
141	
142	\paragraph{The structural mismatch.}
143	The key observation is that factor (b) makes the KL gradient for neuron $j$ depend on \emph{all} other neurons' mask values.
144	BCE's factorized gradient (Eq.~\ref{eq:bce_grad}) optimizes each neuron's prediction accuracy in isolation, but TEAL inference selects neurons via a global ranking across all layers.
145	Under global top-$k$, the decision to retain neuron $j$ depends on the scores of all other neurons, a combinatorial interaction that BCE cannot capture.
146	
147	Concretely, BCE achieves high per-neuron accuracy ($>$90\% oracle agreement) yet produces poor output under TEAL, because it optimizes a proxy misaligned with output preservation under global budget allocation.
148	KL directly optimizes the end-to-end objective, with gradients that reflect inter-neuron dependencies through the computation graph.
149	
150	\paragraph{Empirical verification.}
151	Measuring gradient SNR\footnote{$\text{SNR}_\ell = \|\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]\|_2^2 / \text{Var}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} over 80 steps on LLaMA-3.1-8B (C4 data), KL achieves $5.76\times$ higher average SNR than BCE ($5.77{\times}10^{-5}$ vs.\ $1.00{\times}10^{-5}$), concentrated in early layers (0--4: $34$--$156\times$) where factor~(b)'s Jacobian traverses the full remaining network; late layers (27--31: $1.7$--$3.8\times$) show reduced advantage.
152	BCE's early-layer SNR collapses to ${\sim}10^{-8}$ after step~10, consistent with rapid convergence of per-neuron classification, though an alternative explanation is loss saturation (gradient vanishing once accuracy plateaus).
153	This preliminary measurement persists across learning rates; full-trajectory characterization is left to future work.
154	
155	\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}
156	\label{sec:method:taxonomy}
157	
158	We organize prior work along two axes that capture the essential design decisions for sparsity prediction: \emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies). These axes are motivated by our gradient analysis (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}): granularity determines what information the predictor conditions on, while coupling determines whether the training signal reflects global allocation effects.
159	
160	\begin{table}[t]
161	\centering
162	\caption{Taxonomy of learned sparsity prediction methods along correction granularity (rows) and training signal coupling (columns). Our method fills the previously unexplored Per-token $\times$ Coupled cell.}
163	\label{tab:taxonomy}
164	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
165	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
166	\toprule
167	 & Decomposed (BCE) & Intermediate (MSE) & Coupled (KL) \\
168	\midrule
169	Static & --- & --- & SPON \\
170	Per-token & DejaVu, FastForward & ShadowLLM, D2DMoE & \textbf{Ours} \\
171	\bottomrule
172	\end{tabular}}
173	\end{table}
174	
175	\paragraph{Correction granularity.}
176	\emph{Static} methods~\cite{xu2026spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.
177	\emph{Per-token} methods~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.
178	
179	\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}
180	\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.
181	\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.
182	\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass, reflecting how all mask decisions jointly affect the final distribution.
183	
184	SPON~\cite{xu2026spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\times$ coupled).
185	DejaVu~\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, losing inter-neuron coupling: their decomposed gradient (Eq.~\ref{eq:bce_grad}) is blind to cross-neuron interactions under global allocation.
186	D2DMoE~\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.
187	Our method combines per-token prediction with coupled KL training (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}), propagating gradients through all downstream layers.
188	
189	\begin{figure}[t]
190	\centering
191	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
192	\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}
193	\label{fig:overview}
194	\end{figure}
195	
```

\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:swiglu}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention \\times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.\n\nDefine the gate activation $\\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} = \\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$.\nEach element $g_j^{(\\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.\nWe introduce a binary mask $\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\{0,1\\}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}_{\\text{sparse}}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}.\n\\label{eq:sparse_ffn}\n\\end{align}\nOur goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{h}) \\to $\\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_b}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_b \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ with bottleneck dimension $d_b{=}128$.\nThe predictor takes the hidden state after attention (before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.\n\nEach predictor has $(4096 \\times 128) + (128 independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.\n\n\n\\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}\n\\label{sec:method:gumbel}\n\nThe hard mask $m_j = \\mathbf{1}[s_j > of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\\tau \\to 0$, $\\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.\n\nWe anneal $\\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.\nAt high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons, enabling broad exploration.\nAs temperature decreases, the mask concentrates on confident decisions, 1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We showed that KL distillation resolves the structural mismatch between per-neuron BCE training and global top-$k$ inference allocation, enabling a lightweight predictor ($<$1\% of base) to halve TEAL's perplexity at 50\% sparsity on both LLaMA-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B-v0.3.
5	Uniform per-layer budgets reduce cross-seed downstream standard deviation by $7.5\times$ at 30\% sparsity, providing predictable guarantees; future work includes scaling beyond 7--8B and downstream-aware training objectives.
6	
7

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}
4	TEAL~\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude, achieving 40--50\% sparsity with no training cost.
5	CATS~\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.
6	All training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.
7	
8	\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}
9	DejaVu~\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.
10	ShadowLLM~\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision, providing intermediate coupling within each layer but no end-to-end gradient signal.
11	FastForward~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.
12	SVD-CSP~\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.
13	PowerInfer~\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.
14	MaskLLM~\cite{fang2024maskllm} also employs learnable masks with Gumbel relaxation and frozen weights, but targets N:M semi-structured \emph{weight} sparsity (2:4 patterns) via per-group categorical selection (Gumbel-Softmax), producing fixed masks independent of input tokens.
15	Both share the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but solve distinct optimization problems: MaskLLM finds optimal static N:M patterns within fixed groups, while we predict dynamic per-token masks under a global budget constraint.
16	The structural mismatch we characterize (per-neuron gradient decomposition vs.\ global top-$k$) does not arise in MaskLLM's per-group categorical setting, where N:M patterns eliminate cross-group budget competition.
17	All prior predictor-based methods use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.
18	Our contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.
19	
20	\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}
21	SPON~\cite{xu2026spon} learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.
22	Our method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers, producing input-dependent masks.
23	SPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}
26	ReLU Strikes Back~\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.
27	MiniTron~\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.
28	All require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.
29	
30	\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}
31	Wanda~\cite{sun2024wanda} and SparseGPT~\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt} produce static weight masks; Switch Transformers~\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.
32	KL-based distillation~\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.
33	We apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.
34	
```

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Inference Efficiency}
4	\label{app:latency}
5	
6	Table~\ref{tab:latency} reports inference throughput on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (batch size 1, sequence length 2048, fp16, averaged over 100 forward passes).
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\caption{Inference latency on LLaMA-3.1-8B (RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell, batch=1, seq=2048, fp16, 100 forward passes). Overhead is relative to dense inference.}
11	\label{tab:latency}
12	\small
13	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
14	\toprule
15	Method & Sparsity & ms/fwd & tok/s & Overhead \\
16	\midrule
17	Dense & 0\% & 119.11$\pm$0.57 & 17{,}195 & --- \\
18	\midrule
19	KL predictor-only & 30\% & 126.64$\pm$0.70 & 16{,}172 & +6.3\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
20	KL predictor-only & 50\% & 126.78$\pm$0.31 & 16{,}154 & +6.4\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
21	\midrule
22	Predictor + compensation & 30\% & 129.94$\pm$0.75 & 15{,}761 & +9.1\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
23	Predictor + compensation & 50\% & 129.71$\pm$0.53 & 15{,}789 & +8.9\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	The full KL-sparse pipeline adds 6.4\% overhead to dense inference (126.78 vs.\ 119.11\,ms/forward), of which the predictor's 32-layer forward pass contributes 3.17\,ms (2.7\%); the remainder arises from mask application and sparse FFN indexing.
29	Eliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\sim$2.7\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling), confirming that KL distillation provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.
30	The overhead is near-identical at 30\% and 50\% sparsity because the predictor runs the same computation regardless of target sparsity; only the top-$k$ threshold changes.
31	
32	Note that the current implementation uses unstructured element-wise masks, which do not translate to wall-clock speedup without sparse kernel support.
33	The pipeline adds $\sim$6.4\% overhead; net acceleration requires kernel-level sparse computation optimizations (e.g., Triton block-sparse, cuSPARSE) that are beyond the scope of this work.
34	Our contribution is prediction quality and inference simplification (removing the compensation network), not end-to-end latency reduction.
35	
36	
37	\section{Predictor Bottleneck Sensitivity}
38	\label{app:db_sensitivity}
39	
40	Table~\ref{tab:db_sensitivity} reports the effect of predictor bottleneck dimension $d_b$ on PPL at 50\% sparsity under both TEAL global allocation (the paper's primary inference protocol) and per-token evaluation.
41	
42	\begin{table}[h]
43	\centering
44	\caption{Predictor bottleneck sensitivity at 50\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$). TEAL global is the primary inference protocol; per-token shown for reference.}
45	\label{tab:db_sensitivity}
46	\small
47	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
48	\toprule
49	$d_b$ & Params & TEAL global & $\Delta$ & Per-token \\
50	\midrule
51	64 & 38.2M (0.48\%) & 12.076 & +0.9\% & 10.16 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
52	128 & 75.9M (0.95\%) & 11.963 & --- & 9.218 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
53	256 & 151.5M (1.89\%) & 12.105 & +1.2\% & 8.586 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
54	512 & 302.5M (3.77\%) & 12.315 & +2.9\% & 7.93 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
55	\bottomrule
56	\end{tabular}
57	\end{table}
58	
59	Under TEAL global allocation, increasing $d_b$ beyond 128 provides no benefit (PPL slightly worsens), while per-token evaluation shows monotonic improvement (10.16$\to$7.93).
60	This divergence indicates that the global top-$k$ allocation mechanism, not predictor capacity, is the binding constraint: additional predictor capacity produces better per-neuron scores, but the global ranking and budget distribution cannot exploit them.
61	The $d_b{=}128$ configuration (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base model) is therefore optimal for the TEAL inference protocol.
62	
63	\subsection{Temperature Schedule Sensitivity}
64	\label{app:tau_sensitivity}
65	
66	\begin{table}[h]
67	\centering
68	\caption{Temperature schedule sensitivity at 50\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$, TEAL global).}
69	\label{tab:tau_sensitivity}
70	\small
71	\begin{tabular}{lc}
72	\toprule
73	$\tau$ Schedule & PPL$\downarrow$ \\
74	\midrule
75	Fixed $\tau{=}0.5$ & \textbf{11.861} \\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep
76	Linear $1.0 \to 0.1$ (default) & 11.963 \\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep
77	Fixed $\tau{=}0.1$ & 12.072 \\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep
78	\bottomrule
79	\end{tabular}
80	\end{table}
81	
82	Temperature schedule has minimal impact ($<$1.8\% PPL variation).
83	The linear annealing default is a conservative choice; even removing annealing entirely (fixed $\tau{=}0.5$) yields comparable performance.
84	
85	\subsection{Training Convergence}
86	\label{app:training_convergence}
87	
88	\begin{table}[h]
89	\centering
90	\caption{Training convergence at 50\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$, TEAL global).}
91	\label{tab:training_convergence}
92	\small
93	\begin{tabular}{cc}
94	\toprule
95	Steps & PPL$\downarrow$ \\
96	\midrule
97	200 & 15.158 \\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep
98	400 & 12.538 \\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep
99	600 & 12.046 \\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep
100	800 & 11.907 \\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep
101	1000 & \textbf{11.906} \\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep
102	\bottomrule
103	\end{tabular}
104	\end{table}
105	
106	Training converges rapidly: 95\% of the quality gap closes by step 600, and performance saturates by step 800 ($\Delta < 0.001$ between 800 and 1000 steps).
107	Our default of 1000 steps provides a conservative margin.
108	
109	
110	\section{Adaptive Penalty Controller Dynamics}
111	\label{app:lagrangian}
112	
113	Table~\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the adaptive penalty controller across sparsity targets.
114	The geometric doubling pattern ($1 \to 2 \to 4 \to 8 \to \ldots$) enables $\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\pm$2\% of the target.
115	
116	\begin{table}[h]
117	\centering
118	\caption{Penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets (multiplicative schedule, margin $\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}
119	\label{tab:lagrangian}
120	\small
121	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
122	\toprule
123	Target $\rho$ & $\lambda_{\max}$ & Final $\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\
124	\midrule
125	30\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\% \\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42
126	40\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\% \\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42
127	50\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\% \\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42
128	70\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\% \\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42
129	\bottomrule
130	\end{tabular}
131	\end{table}
132	
133	At 50\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.
134	At 30\% and 70\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\lambda$ to its respective caps.
135	The 40\% target converges to $\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\% deviation, illustrating the controller's precision when the target is reachable without saturating.
136	
137	\begin{figure}[h]
138	\centering
139	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}
140	\caption{Adaptive penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets. Upper panel: penalty weight $\lambda$ (log scale) over training steps. The 30\% target requires extreme penalty ($\lambda \to 5000$) to suppress activations below their natural density, while 50\% needs only $\lambda \approx 8$ since SwiGLU gates already cluster near this sparsity. Lower panel: actual sparsity (solid) converges to the target (dashed) within $\sim$10 steps via geometric doubling.}
141	\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	Temperature $\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.
145	A mild loss increase in the final $\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\tau \to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, confirming the training is converging toward the hard-mask inference regime.
146	
147	The controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.
148	
149	
150	\section{Out-of-Distribution Evaluation}
151	\label{app:ood}
152	
153	To address concerns about data homogeneity between training (WikiText-103) and evaluation (WikiText-2), we evaluate on C4~\cite{raffel2020c4} validation set at 50\% sparsity under TEAL global allocation.
154	
155	\begin{table}[h]
156	\centering
157	\caption{Out-of-distribution evaluation on C4 validation (50\% sparsity, PPL$\downarrow$).}
158	\label{tab:ood}
159	\small
160	\begin{tabular}{lc}
161	\toprule
162	Method & C4 PPL$\downarrow$ \\
163	\midrule
164	Dense & 9.21 \\
165	KL predictor (Ours) & \textbf{18.40} \\ % exp_id: eval_c4_ood_kl50
166	TEAL vanilla & 38.75 \\
167	\bottomrule
168	\end{tabular}
169	\end{table}
170	
171	The KL-trained predictor achieves 52.5\% relative advantage over TEAL on out-of-domain data (18.40 vs.\ 38.75), comparable to or slightly exceeding the 49\% advantage observed on WikiText-2 (11.79 vs.\ 23.31), suggesting that KL-trained predictions generalize to held-out data when the evaluation distribution differs from training.
172	This confirms that the predictor learns generalizable neuron importance patterns rather than overfitting to the WikiText training distribution.
173	
174	
175	\section{Per-Layer Retention Analysis}
176	\label{app:retention}
177

the remainder arises from mask application and sparse FFN indexing.\nEliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\\sim$2.7\\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling), confirming that KL distillation provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.\nThe overhead is near-identical at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity because the predictor runs the same computation regardless of target sparsity; ```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\% sparsity achieves PPL 8.36 but MMLU already degrades, motivating 30\% as the recommended operating point for downstream fidelity.
4	Training requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.
5	All experiments use 7--8B parameter models (LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B-v0.3); scaling behavior to 13B or 70B models remains untested.
6	Out-of-distribution evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}) covers only 50\% sparsity on C4; extending to 30\% and 70\% is left to future work.
7	Our attribution of KL's advantage to gradient coupling (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}) is supported by SNR measurements and the BCE isolation ablation, but does not fully disentangle coupling from oracle quality: BCE uses magnitude-based oracles while KL directly optimizes output preservation, and an ablation using KL-derived masks as BCE targets would more precisely isolate the coupling effect.
8	At 30\% sparsity, global allocation exhibits 9.5pp cross-seed MMLU standard deviation (3-seed), indicating that allocation strategy interacts with predictor initialization in task-specific ways; this variance is MMLU-specific, as ARC, WinoGrande, and GSM8K show stable performance across seeds under both allocation strategies.
9	While we motivate our work with inference speedup potential, actual wall-clock acceleration depends on sparse kernel implementations (e.g., Triton, cuSPARSE) and hardware-specific memory access patterns; this work focuses on prediction quality rather than systems optimization.
10	Future directions include N:M structured sparsity for hardware-native acceleration, scaling to 13B+ models, and training a single predictor that serves multiple sparsity targets simultaneously.
11	
```

    "originalFile": "We study whether end-to-end KL distillation can replace per-neuron classification for activation sparsity prediction in SwiGLU LLMs.\nExisting predictors trained with binary cross-entropy (BCE) optimize each neuron independently, but inference allocates sparsity budgets via global top-$k$ across all layers.\nGradient analysis reveals a structural mismatch: BCE's per-neuron gradient is blind to cross-neuron interactions, achieving higher oracle F1 yet 137\\% worse perplexity than a KL-trained predictor.\nA lightweight predictor ($<$1\\% of base parameters) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation resolves this mismatch by coupling all neurons through the output distribution.\nAt the recommended 30\\% sparsity operating point on LLaMA-3.1-8B, KL distillation reduces PPL degradation by 18\\% over magnitude-based TEAL; uniform allocation provides $7.5\\times$ smaller cross-seed downstream standard deviation than global top-$k$, eliminating seed-dependent performance instability.\nAt higher sparsity (50\\%), PPL advantage widens (49\\% over TEAL) but downstream tasks degrade regardless of allocation strategy, revealing allocation-dependent operating regions at the 7--8B scale.\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 confirms consistent improvements (within 8\\% ratio at $\\leq$50\\% sparsity).\n",

  "tool_use_result": {

    "oldString": "\\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity, revealing that oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled: BCE achieves +7pp F1 yet 137\\% worse PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
    "newString": "\\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity: oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled, with BCE at +7pp F1 yet 137\\% worse PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: TEAL's top-$k$ mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal} ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\n\nWe provide this analysis.\nThe gradient of BCE with respect to neuron $j$'s score is $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$, a function of $j$ alone, independent of every other neuron.\nUnder KL divergence, the gradient for neuron $j$ in layer $\\ell$ factors as:\n\\begin{equation*}\n\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}}}{\\partial s_j^{(\\ell)}} = \\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}}}{\\partial \\mathbf{z}} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial \\mathbf{z}}{\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}}{\\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}} \\cdot \\frac{\\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}}{\\partial s_j^{(\\ell)}},\n\\end{equation*}\nwhere the Jacobian $\\partial \\mathbf{z} / \\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}$ propagates through all downstream layers, coupling each neuron's gradient to every other neuron's mask state.\nThis structural difference predicts that KL should outperform BCE specifically under global allocation, and the prediction is confirmed empirically: BCE achieves higher oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\\ 0.540) yet produces 137\\% worse perplexity, because per-neuron magnitude accuracy is a misaligned proxy for output-distribution preservation.\n\nTo test whether end-to-end distillation can overcome this mismatch, we instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nWith uniform allocation, the predictor achieves PPL 11.96 at 50\\% sparsity; under global top-$k$, PPL improves to 11.79 (49\\% over TEAL) but late-layer starvation amplifies cross-seed downstream standard deviation ($7.5\\times$), motivating uniform allocation for deployment.\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} confirms consistent improvements at the 7--8B scale (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy} situates prior work along correction granularity and training signal coupling).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item Quantitative characterization of the structural mismatch between per-neuron BCE training and global top-$k$ inference allocation, supported by oracle F1 decomposition and gradient SNR measurements (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\n\\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity, revealing that oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled: BCE achieves +7pp F1 yet 137\\% worse PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Analysis of how allocation strategy governs the prediction-to-downstream gap, identifying a late-layer starvation mechanism and characterizing qualitative shifts across sparsity levels at the 7--8B scale (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

          "-\\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity, revealing that oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled: BCE achieves +7pp F1 yet 137\\% worse PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
          "+\\item Empirical validation that KL distillation overcomes this mismatch for activation sparsity: oracle F1 and perplexity are inversely decoupled, with BCE at +7pp F1 yet 137\\% worse PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
          " \\item Analysis of how allocation strategy governs the prediction-to-downstream gap, identifying a late-layer starvation mechanism and characterizing qualitative shifts across sparsity levels at the 7--8B scale (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).",
