    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}\nWe evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.\nThe sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \\to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \\to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nAll predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4, using AdamW~\\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $5 \\times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.\nThe KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation.}\nWe report perplexity on WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), and GSM8K (8-shot).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}\nAt inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.\nWe term this \\textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare against: (1) \\textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly; (2) \\textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; (3) \\textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers; and (4) \\textbf{WINA}~\\cite{liu2025teal}, an importance-weighted variant.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. We recommend uniform allocation for deployment (best downstream preservation); global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nAt 30\\% sparsity, KL distillation with global TEAL allocation achieves PPL 8.98 and reduces TEAL's degradation from dense (10.97 $\\to$ 8.98, an 18\\% improvement). % exp_id: kl_30_global_teal\nThe advantage holds across additional downstream benchmarks (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\nUniform allocation at 30\\% provides stable downstream performance (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed) while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\\pm$9.5\\%), with late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\\%) interacting with predictor initialization (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_3seed\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, KL distillation over vanilla TEAL widens the PPL advantage: 11.79 vs.\\ 23.31, a 49\\% reduction. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bench_teal50\nThe predictor also generalizes to held-out data (C4 validation: 52.5\\% advantage over TEAL; see Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nHowever, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\\pm$0.2\\% vs.\\ 28.0$\\pm$0.6\\%, both $\\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\\%); this is a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results on LLaMA-3.1-8B. PPL$\\downarrow$ on WikiText-2; accuracy$\\uparrow$ on downstream tasks. \\textbf{Bold}: best among compensation-free methods per sparsity level. BCE follows the DejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} predictor pipeline (per-neuron BCE on magnitude oracles) with a FastForward-style~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} compensation network (67.2M params) applied post-masking; no TEAL allocation. KL MMLU at 30\\% and 50\\% reports 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; 50\\% uniform PPL is 3-seed ($11.92 \\pm 0.07$; Appendix~\\ref{app:stability}); all other entries use seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:main}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & \\textbf{42.32} & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30\n& KL global (Ours) & 8.98 & 39.68 & \\textbf{70.17} & 35.7$\\pm$9.5 & \\textbf{8.79} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.82} & 40.78 & 68.59 & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} & 7.35 \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{50\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \\textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50\n& KL global (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{32.85} & \\textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.12} \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n& BCE (2700 steps)$^\\ast$ & 27.97 & 23.98 & 50.59 & 30.02 & 4.85 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}\n\\label{sec:isocompute}\n\nTo test whether BCE simply requires more compute, we train it for 2700 steps (2.71$\\times$ the KL step budget, approximately equal wall-clock time: $\\sim$46 min BCE vs.\\ $\\sim$45 min KL on RTX PRO 6000).\nThe BCE predictor achieves PPL 27.97, still 2.37$\\times$ worse than KL at 1000 steps (PPL 11.79). % exp_id: bce_isocompute_s42\nThe failure is structural, not due to architectural or compute differences: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\\ 0.540) with 137\\% worse PPL (27.97 vs.\\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) confirms it learns a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude matching. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42\nFuture work could further isolate coupling effects through MSE on hidden states and per-layer KL ablations.\n\n\n\\subsection{Ablation Studies}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 50\\% sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Ablation at 50\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, unified benchmark protocol).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & PPL$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$ from Ours \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{KL-based variants}} \\\\\nForward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & --- \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global\nJSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 12.54 & +0.75 \\\\ % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42\nReverse KL + Gumbel & 12.80 & +1.01 \\\\ % exp_id: reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nForward KL + STE & 15.77 & +3.98 \\\\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\\\\nBCE + Compensation (hard mask) & 25.44 & +13.65 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_comp_staged_s42\nBCE (no compensation) & 27.97 & +16.18 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42\nBCE + Gumbel + Penalty (no KL) & 38.02 & +26.23 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Gumbel vs.\\ STE.}\nReplacing Gumbel-Sigmoid with STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} degrades PPL from 11.79 to 15.77 (+3.98), accounting for 34.5\\% of the improvement over vanilla TEAL. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Divergence Direction.}\nForward KL (mode-covering) outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with the SNR analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}: complete teacher supervision enables better global allocation learning. % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nThis contrasts with MiniLLM~\\cite{gu2024minillm}, which finds reverse KL superior for sequence-level distillation: in generation, mode-seeking prevents low-probability token production, while in mask prediction, mode-covering (forward KL) ensures no important neuron is omitted ($q(x) \\approx 0$ where $p(x) > 0$ is directly penalized).\n\n\\paragraph{SPON Contribution.}\nAdding SPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} bias correction reduces PPL from 12.08 to 11.79 (2.4\\% relative); see \\S\\ref{sec:spon} for decomposition.\\footnote{SPON analysis uses \\texttt{evaluate.py}; main results use the unified benchmark.} % exp_id: spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Loss Function Isolation.}\nBCE with identical Gumbel-Sigmoid and penalty yields PPL 38.02, worse than vanilla BCE (27.97): the penalty interferes with per-neuron optimization without global coordination. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\nThe loss effect ($\\Delta$PPL=26.23) is $6.6\\times$ the relaxation effect ($\\Delta$PPL=3.98).\n\n\\paragraph{BCE + Compensation.}\nFastForward-style compensation~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} improves BCE from 27.97 to 25.44 (9.0\\%), still $2.16\\times$ worse than KL. % exp_id: bce_comp_staged_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameter Robustness.}\nAppendix~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity} confirms robustness to predictor capacity ($d_b$, $<$3\\%), temperature ($<$1.8\\%), and training steps (saturated by 800). % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep\n\n\n\\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scaling} and Figure~\\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep} show PPL as a function of sparsity for the KL predictor under global allocation and vanilla TEAL.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}\n\\caption{Perplexity scaling with sparsity. The KL predictor's advantage grows disproportionately as more neurons are pruned.}\n\\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{PPL scaling with sparsity (WikiText-2). KL advantage grows disproportionately with sparsity.}\n\\label{tab:scaling}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & KL (Ours) & TEAL vanilla & Relative advantage \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & \\textbf{8.98} & 10.97 & 18\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30_global_teal\n50\\% & \\textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 49\\% \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global\n70\\% & \\textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 82\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70_global_teal\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL advantage grows from 18\\% at 30\\% to 82\\% at 70\\%, where TEAL collapses (PPL 100.11) while the learned predictor maintains coherent allocation.\nIn absolute terms, KL/Dense degradation is $1.47\\times$ at 30\\%, $1.93\\times$ at 50\\%, and $2.93\\times$ at 70\\%, compared to TEAL/Dense ratios of $1.79\\times$, $3.81\\times$, and $16.4\\times$ respectively.\n\n\\paragraph{Three Operating Regions.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase} presents global vs.\\ uniform allocation at three sparsity levels and reveals qualitative shifts in allocation preference. Denser sampling (e.g., 35\\% sparsity) would be needed to precisely locate region boundaries.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Allocation analysis: global (TEAL) vs.\\ uniform per-layer allocation across sparsity levels. 30\\% and 50\\% KL rows report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std for MMLU; other metrics use seed 42. Each row uses a predictor trained at the respective sparsity target.}\n\\label{tab:phase}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Allocation & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & \\textbf{42.32} & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30\n& KL global & 8.98 & 39.68 & \\textbf{70.17} & 35.7$\\pm$9.5 & \\textbf{8.79} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.82} & 40.78 & 68.59 & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} & 7.35 \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{40\\%}\n& KL global & 10.12 & 40.96 & 65.35 & \\textbf{38.06} & \\textbf{5.00} \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl40_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.36} & \\textbf{41.81} & \\textbf{67.17} & 30.68 & 3.94 \\\\ % exp_id: d030_kl40_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{50\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \\textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50\n& KL global & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{32.85} & \\textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.12} \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe allocation preference is \\emph{non-monotonic}. We observe three qualitative behaviors at the sampled sparsity levels (7--8B scale):\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Budget-surplus ($\\leq$30\\%).}\nUniform allocation provides $7.5\\times$ smaller cross-seed MMLU standard deviation ($\\sigma$: 1.3pp vs.\\ 9.5pp), eliminating catastrophic seed-dependent degradation under global top-$k$ (MMLU 35.7$\\pm$9.5\\% vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed). Global top-$k$ starves late layers (L31: 28.4\\%, std/mean$=$1.89), and this starvation interacts unpredictably with predictor initialization (Appendix~\\ref{app:lagrangian}). % exp_id: kl30_3seed\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Budget-constrained ($\\sim$40\\%).}\nThe preference reverses: global achieves MMLU 38.06\\% vs.\\ uniform's 30.68\\% (+7.4pp). % exp_id: bench_kl40_global, d030_kl40_uniform\nPPL remains low (8.36) but downstream accuracy degrades; PPL is a poor proxy for reasoning.\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Capacity-limited ($\\geq$50\\%).}\nGlobal/uniform gap vanishes (MMLU 28.1$\\pm$0.2\\% vs.\\ 28.0$\\pm$0.6\\%, $\\sigma\\leq$0.6pp; contrast 9.5pp at 30\\%), consistent with capacity limitation. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform\nLate-layer starvation persists across levels (L24--31 retention: 50.1/34.2/22.6\\% at 30/50/70\\% sparsity; Figure~\\ref{fig:retention}), consistent on both 7--8B models (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_arch}).\n\n\\paragraph{Gap Decomposition.}\nUnder uniform allocation, 53\\% of the Dense$\\to$KL gap is predictor approximation, 47\\% budget allocation (Appendix~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity}); under global, KL remains $1.98\\times$ better than TEAL.\n\n\n\\subsection{SPON Orthogonality Analysis}\n\\label{sec:spon}\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, the predictor's hidden-state perturbation (L2=482.65) and SPON's additive correction (L2=76.2, cosine similarity 0.196) are weakly correlated ($R^2$=0.038); SPON yields only 2.4\\% PPL improvement (12.08$\\to$11.79); the KL predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture Validation.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch} evaluates on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (GQA + sliding window, dense PPL 5.52).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). Adv = TEAL\\,/\\,KL ratio. LLaMA uses global top-$k$; Mistral uses uniform allocation (matched within each architecture).}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{l@{\\hspace{4pt}}cc@{\\hspace{3pt}}c@{\\hspace{6pt}}|@{\\hspace{6pt}}cc@{\\hspace{3pt}}c}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{Mistral-7B} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{LLaMA-8B} \\\\\n & KL & TEAL & Adv & KL & TEAL & Adv \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & \\textbf{6.77} & 8.38 & 1.24$\\times$ & \\textbf{8.98} & 10.97 & 1.22$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30_mistral, teal_30_mistral\n50\\% & \\textbf{8.65} & 18.31 & 2.12$\\times$ & \\textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 1.98$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50_mistral, teal_50_mistral\n70\\% & \\textbf{13.33} & 61.72 & 4.63$\\times$ & \\textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 5.59$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70_mistral, teal_70_mistral\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL advantage ratio is consistent across architectures (within 8\\% at $\\leq$50\\% sparsity; 4.63$\\times$ vs.\\ 5.59$\\times$ at 70\\%) and provides preliminary evidence of generality at the 7--8B scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale Generalization: Qwen-2.5-14B.}\nOn Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} (dense PPL 5.18), KL achieves PPL 7.62 at 30\\% ($1.47\\times$ dense, matching the 8B ratio); BCE collapses to 6.4\\% native sparsity ($10\\times$ stronger penalty also fails), consistent with architecture-general gradient insufficiency (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}). % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42_v2\n% TODO: add vanilla TEAL 14B PPL when qwen14b_teal30_eval completes\n\n\n\\paragraph{Inference Efficiency.}\nThe pipeline adds 6.4\\% overhead to dense inference while removing the compensation network ($-$2.7\\% latency, $-$67.2M params); post-hoc SPON yields $<$3\\% additional gain (\\S\\ref{sec:spon}), confirming the predictor internalizes most error-correction signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\n\\paragraph{Training Stability.}\nThree-seed evaluation (50\\%): PPL $11.92 \\pm 0.07$, MMLU $\\sigma\\leq$0.6pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:stability}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present a KL distillation framework for training contextual sparsity predictors in SwiGLU-based language models.\nThe method replaces per-neuron binary classification (BCE) with an output-distribution objective that couples all pruning decisions through the model's forward pass, enabling competitive performance without auxiliary error compensation networks at inference.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:method:formulation}\n\nConsider a pretrained transformer with $L$ layers, each containing a SwiGLU feed-forward network~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}.\nFor layer $\\ell$, the FFN computes:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:swiglu}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention ($d{=}4096$), $\\sigma$ denotes SiLU, $\\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}, \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}} \\times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.\n\nDefine the gate activation $\\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} = \\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$.\nEach element $g_j^{(\\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.\nWe introduce a binary mask $\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\{0,1\\}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}_{\\text{sparse}}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}.\n\\label{eq:sparse_ffn}\n\\end{align}\nOur goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{h}) \\to \\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)} \\in (0,1)^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that produces per-neuron importance scores from the pre-FFN hidden state $\\mathbf{h}$.\nAt inference, the scores are fed to a global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal} that distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly, retaining the $(1{-}\\rho) \\times L \\times d_{\\text{ff}}$ highest-scoring neurons model-wide for a target sparsity ratio $\\rho$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Predictor Architecture}\n\\label{sec:method:predictor}\n\nEach layer $\\ell$ has an independent two-layer MLP predictor:\n\\begin{align}\nf_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{h}) = \\text{GELU}(\\mathbf{h} \\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)}) \\, \\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:predictor}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_b}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_b \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ with bottleneck dimension $d_b{=}128$.\nThe predictor takes the hidden state after attention (before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.\n\nEach predictor has $(4096 \\times 128) + (128 \\times 14336) = 2.37\\text{M}$ parameters.\nWith 32 independent predictors (one per layer), the total overhead is 75.9M parameters, less than 1\\% of the 8B base model.\nThe predictors operate independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.\n\n\n\\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}\n\\label{sec:method:gumbel}\n\nThe hard mask $m_j = \\mathbf{1}[s_j > \\text{threshold}]$ is non-differentiable, preventing gradient flow from the KL objective through the mask to the predictor parameters.\nWe apply Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} to produce differentiable soft masks:\n\\begin{align}\n\\tilde{m}_j = \\sigma_\\tau\\!\\left(\\log \\frac{s_j}{1 - s_j} + g_1 - g_2\\right), \\quad g_1, g_2 \\sim \\text{Gumbel}(0,1),\n\\label{eq:gumbel}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\sigma_\\tau(x) = \\text{sigmoid}(x / \\tau)$ and $\\tau$ is a temperature parameter.\nThe Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\\tau \\to 0$, $\\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.\n\nWe anneal $\\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.\nAt high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons, enabling broad exploration.\nAs temperature decreases, the mask concentrates on confident decisions, approximating the hard allocation used at inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison with Straight-Through Estimation.}\nThe straight-through estimator (STE)~\\cite{bengio2013ste} applies a hard threshold in the forward pass and passes gradients through unchanged ($\\partial m / \\partial s = 1$).\nIn our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously, causing violent oscillations in the penalty weight and failure to converge to the target sparsity.\nGumbel relaxation eliminates this pathology, contributing a 34.5\\% improvement (PPL 15.77 $\\to$ 11.79 at 50\\% sparsity), as we verify empirically in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}.\n\n\n\\subsection{KL Divergence Training Objective}\n\\label{sec:method:kl}\n\nLet $\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{dense}}$ denote the output logits from a full dense forward pass, and $\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}$ the logits from a forward pass with predictor-generated soft masks $\\{\\tilde{\\mathbf{m}}^{(\\ell)}\\}_\\ell$ applied at every layer.\nWe minimize the forward KL divergence between the dense and sparse output distributions:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} = D_{\\text{KL}}(p_{\\text{dense}} \\,\\|\\, p_{\\text{sparse}}) = \\sum_{v} p_{\\text{dense}}^{(v)} \\log \\frac{p_{\\text{dense}}^{(v)}}{p_{\\text{sparse}}^{(v)}},\n\\label{eq:kl_loss}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $p_{\\text{dense}} = \\text{softmax}(\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{dense}})$ and $p_{\\text{sparse}} = \\text{softmax}(\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}})$, summed over the vocabulary $v$ and averaged over sequence positions.\nThe base model is frozen; only the predictor parameters receive gradients.\n\nWe use forward KL (mean-seeking) rather than reverse KL, as the goal is to preserve the dense model's primary predictions rather than explore alternative modes; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} validates this choice with ablations over divergence direction (forward KL outperforms JSD by 6.4\\% and reverse KL by 8.6\\%).\nNo temperature scaling ($T{=}1$) is applied~\\cite{hinton2015distilling}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Adaptive Penalty Sparsity Controller}\n\\label{sec:method:lagrangian}\n\nWe enforce a model-level sparsity target $\\rho$ via constrained optimization.\nLet $\\bar{s} = \\frac{1}{L} \\sum_\\ell \\frac{1}{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\sum_j \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}$ denote the average fraction of retained neurons.\nThe constrained problem is:\n\\begin{align}\n\\min_\\theta \\; \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\bar{s} \\leq 1 - \\rho.\n\\label{eq:constrained}\n\\end{align}\nWe relax this via an adaptive penalty controller:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} + \\lambda \\cdot \\text{clamp}(|\\bar{s} - (1 - \\rho)| - \\epsilon, \\min{=}0)^2,\n\\label{eq:lagrangian}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\epsilon = 0.02$ is a deadzone margin that prevents $\\lambda$ from responding to small constraint violations.\nThe penalty weight $\\lambda$ is updated multiplicatively at each step:\n\\begin{align}\n\\lambda \\leftarrow \\begin{cases} \\lambda \\times 2 & \\text{if } \\bar{s} > (1-\\rho) + \\epsilon \\\\ \\lambda \\times 0.5 & \\text{if } \\bar{s} < (1-\\rho) - \\epsilon \\\\ \\lambda & \\text{otherwise} \\end{cases}\n\\label{eq:dual_update}\n\\end{align}\nwith $\\lambda$ clamped to $[\\lambda_{\\text{floor}}, \\lambda_{\\max}]$ where $\\lambda_{\\text{floor}} = 0.01$ and $\\lambda_{\\max} \\in \\{1000, 5000\\}$ depending on the target.\nThe geometric doubling requires only $\\lambda_{\\max}$ as a user-specified hyperparameter (no step-size tuning) and reaches target sparsity within 40--110 steps for 40--50\\% targets; for 30\\% and 70\\% targets, $\\lambda$ saturates at $\\lambda_{\\max}$ with 1.7--5.0\\% residual deviation (Appendix~\\ref{app:lagrangian}, Figure~\\ref{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}). The deadzone prevents oscillation once sparsity is within $\\pm$2\\%.\\footnote{Our update rule resembles the augmented Lagrangian method but replaces gradient-based dual updates with a multiplicative schedule for training stability. We term it an adaptive penalty controller to distinguish from formal dual methods that guarantee KKT satisfaction.}\n\n\n\\subsection{Gradient-Based Motivation: BCE vs.\\ KL}\n\\label{sec:method:gradient}\n\nWe analyze the structural difference between BCE and KL training signals to motivate why KL distillation is better suited to global top-$k$ inference.\n\n\\paragraph{BCE gradient structure.}\nThe standard approach trains the predictor against oracle masks $m_j^* = \\mathbf{1}[|g_j| \\geq \\text{top-}k\\text{ threshold}]$ derived from the dense model's activation magnitudes, where the threshold is the global top-$k$ cutoff across all layers (matching TEAL's inference protocol).\nThe loss decomposes per neuron:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} = -\\frac{1}{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\sum_{j=1}^{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\bigl[ m_j^* \\log \\sigma(s_j) + (1 - m_j^*) \\log(1 - \\sigma(s_j)) \\bigr].\n\\label{eq:bce}\n\\end{align}\nThe gradient with respect to predictor logit $s_j$ is:\n\\begin{align}\n\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}}}{\\partial s_j} = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*.\n\\label{eq:bce_grad}\n\\end{align}\nThis gradient depends \\emph{only} on neuron $j$'s own prediction and target.\nEach neuron is trained independently; there is no inter-neuron coupling in the learning signal.\n\n\\paragraph{KL gradient structure.}\nUnder KL distillation, the gradient for predictor logit $s_j^{(\\ell)}$ in layer $\\ell$ is:\n\\begin{align}\n\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}}}{\\partial s_j^{(\\ell)}} = \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}}}{\\partial \\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}}}_{\\text{(a)}} \\cdot \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}}{\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}}}_{\\text{(b)}} \\cdot \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}}{\\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}}}_{\\text{(c)}} \\cdot \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}}{\\partial s_j^{(\\ell)}}}_{\\text{(d)}},\n\\label{eq:kl_grad}\n\\end{align}\nwhere each factor has a distinct role:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{(a)}] The KL gradient at the output, reflecting how each logit component deviates from the dense distribution.\n\\item[\\textbf{(b)}] The Jacobian through all downstream layers from layer $\\ell$'s output to the final logits. This factor couples neuron $j$'s gradient to the state of every other retained neuron in layers $\\ell, \\ell{+}1, \\ldots, L$, because the sparse hidden state propagates through subsequent attention and FFN operations.\n\\item[\\textbf{(c)}] The local mask effect: $\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)} / \\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)} = (g_j^{(\\ell)} \\cdot [\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}]_j) \\cdot \\mathbf{W}_{d,j}^{(\\ell)}$, projecting through the down-projection to affect the full $d$-dimensional hidden state.\n\\item[\\textbf{(d)}] The Gumbel-Sigmoid derivative, providing the differentiable path from logit to mask.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{The structural mismatch.}\nThe key observation is that factor (b) makes the KL gradient for neuron $j$ depend on \\emph{all} other neurons' mask values.\nBCE's factorized gradient (Eq.~\\ref{eq:bce_grad}) optimizes each neuron's prediction accuracy in isolation, but TEAL inference selects neurons via a global ranking across all layers.\nUnder global top-$k$, the decision to retain neuron $j$ depends on the scores of all other neurons, a combinatorial interaction that BCE cannot capture.\n\nConcretely, BCE achieves high per-neuron accuracy ($>$90\\% oracle agreement) yet produces poor output under TEAL, because it optimizes a proxy misaligned with output preservation under global budget allocation.\nKL directly optimizes the end-to-end objective, with gradients that reflect inter-neuron dependencies through the computation graph.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical verification.}\nMeasuring gradient SNR\\footnote{$\\text{SNR}_\\ell = \\|\\mathbb{E}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]\\|_2^2 / \\text{Var}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} over 80 steps on LLaMA-3.1-8B (C4 data), KL achieves $5.76\\times$ higher average SNR than BCE ($5.77{\\times}10^{-5}$ vs.\\ $1.00{\\times}10^{-5}$), concentrated in early layers (0--4: $34$--$156\\times$) where factor~(b)'s Jacobian traverses the full remaining network; late layers (27--31: $1.7$--$3.8\\times$) show reduced advantage.\nBCE's early-layer SNR collapses to ${\\sim}10^{-8}$ after step~10, consistent with rapid convergence of per-neuron classification, though an alternative explanation is loss saturation (gradient vanishing once accuracy plateaus).\nThis preliminary measurement persists across learning rates; full-trajectory characterization is left to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}\n\\label{sec:method:taxonomy}\n\nWe organize prior work along two axes that capture the essential design decisions for sparsity prediction: \\emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \\emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies). These axes are motivated by our gradient analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}): granularity determines what information the predictor conditions on, while coupling determines whether the training signal reflects global allocation effects.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Taxonomy of learned sparsity prediction methods along correction granularity (rows) and training signal coupling (columns). Our method fills the previously unexplored Per-token $\\times$ Coupled cell.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & Decomposed (BCE) & Intermediate (MSE) & Coupled (KL) \\\\\n\\midrule\nStatic & --- & --- & SPON \\\\\nPer-token & DejaVu, FastForward & ShadowLLM, D2DMoE & \\textbf{Ours} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Correction granularity.}\n\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2026spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.\n\\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}\n\\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.\n\\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.\n\\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass, reflecting how all mask decisions jointly affect the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, losing inter-neuron coupling: their decomposed gradient (Eq.~\\ref{eq:bce_grad}) is blind to cross-neuron interactions under global allocation.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nOur method combines per-token prediction with coupled KL training (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}), propagating gradients through all downstream layers.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",

feed-forward network~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}.\nFor layer $\\ell$, the FFN computes:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:swiglu}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention ($d{=}4096$), $\\sigma$ denotes SiLU, $\\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}, \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}} \\times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.\n\nDefine the gate activation $\\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} = \\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$.\nEach element $g_j^{(\\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.\nWe introduce a binary mask $\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\{0,1\\}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}_{\\text{sparse}}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}.\n\\label{eq:sparse_ffn}\n\\end{align}\nOur goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{h}) \\to hidden state after attention (before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.\n\nEach predictor has $(4096 \\times 128) + (128 \\times 14336) = 2.37\\text{M}$ parameters.\nWith 32 independent predictors (one per layer), the total overhead is 75.9M parameters, less than 1\\% of the 8B base model.\nThe predictors operate independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.\n\n\n\\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}\n\\label{sec:method:gumbel}\n\nThe hard mask $m_j = \\mathbf{1}[s_j > \\text{threshold}]$ is non-differentiable, preventing gradient flow from the KL objective through the mask to the predictor parameters.\nWe apply Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} to verification.}\nMeasuring gradient SNR\\footnote{$\\text{SNR}_\\ell = \\|\\mathbb{E}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]\\|_2^2 / \\text{Var}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} over 80 steps on LLaMA-3.1-8B (C4 data), KL achieves $5.76\\times$ higher average SNR than BCE ($5.77{\\times}10^{-5}$ vs.\\ $1.00{\\times}10^{-5}$), concentrated in early layers (0--4: $34$--$156\\times$) where factor~(b)'s Jacobian traverses the full remaining network; late layers (27--31: $1.7$--$3.8\\times$) show reduced advantage.\nBCE's early-layer SNR collapses to ${\\sim}10^{-8}$ after step~10, consistent with rapid convergence of per-neuron classification, though an alternative explanation is loss saturation (gradient vanishing once accuracy plateaus).\nThis preliminary measurement persists across learning rates; full-trajectory characterization is left to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}\n\\label{sec:method:taxonomy}\n\nWe organize prior work along two axes that capture the essential design decisions for sparsity prediction: \\emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \\emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies). These axes are motivated by our gradient analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}): granularity determines what information the predictor conditions on, while coupling determines whether the training signal reflects global allocation effects.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Taxonomy of learned sparsity prediction methods along correction granularity (rows) and training signal coupling (columns). Our method fills the previously unexplored Per-token $\\times$ Coupled cell.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n & Decomposed (BCE) & Intermediate (MSE) & Coupled (KL) \\\\\n\\midrule\nStatic & --- & --- & SPON \\\\\nPer-token & DejaVu, FastForward & ShadowLLM, D2DMoE & \\textbf{Ours} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Correction granularity.}\n\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2026spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.\n\\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}\n\\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.\n\\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.\n\\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass, reflecting how all mask decisions jointly affect the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, losing inter-neuron coupling: their decomposed gradient (Eq.~\\ref{eq:bce_grad}) is blind to cross-neuron interactions under global allocation.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nOur method combines per-token prediction with coupled KL training (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}), propagating gradients through all downstream layers.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe present a KL distillation framework for training contextual sparsity predictors in SwiGLU-based language models.\nThe method replaces per-neuron binary classification (BCE) with an output-distribution objective that couples all pruning decisions through the model's forward pass and achieves competitive performance without auxiliary error compensation networks at inference.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:method:formulation}\n\nConsider a pretrained transformer with $L$ layers, each containing a SwiGLU feed-forward network~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}.\nFor layer $\\ell$, the FFN computes:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:swiglu}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention ($d{=}4096$), $\\sigma$ denotes SiLU, $\\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}, \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}} \\times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.\n\nDefine the gate activation $\\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} = \\sigma(\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_g^{(\\ell)}) \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$.\nEach element $g_j^{(\\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.\nWe introduce a binary mask $\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\in \\{0,1\\}^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{y}_{\\text{sparse}}^{(\\ell)} = \\bigl(\\mathbf{m}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}\\bigr) \\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}.\n\\label{eq:sparse_ffn}\n\\end{align}\nOur goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{h}) \\to \\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)} \\in (0,1)^{d_{\\text{ff}}}$ that produces per-neuron importance scores from the pre-FFN hidden state $\\mathbf{h}$.\nAt inference, the scores are fed to a global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal} that distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly, retaining the $(1{-}\\rho) \\times L \\times d_{\\text{ff}}$ highest-scoring neurons model-wide for a target sparsity ratio $\\rho$.\n\n\n\\subsection{Predictor Architecture}\n\\label{sec:method:predictor}\n\nEach layer $\\ell$ has an independent two-layer MLP predictor:\n\\begin{align}\nf_\\theta^{(\\ell)}(\\mathbf{h}) = \\text{GELU}(\\mathbf{h} \\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)}) \\, \\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)},\n\\label{eq:predictor}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\mathbf{W}_1^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d \\times d_b}$ and $\\mathbf{W}_2^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^{d_b \\times d_{\\text{ff}}}$ with bottleneck dimension $d_b{=}128$.\nThe predictor takes the hidden state after attention (before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\\mathbf{s}^{(\\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.\n\nEach predictor has $(4096 \\times 128) + (128 \\times 14336) = 2.37\\text{M}$ parameters.\nWith 32 independent predictors (one per layer), the total overhead is 75.9M parameters, less than 1\\% of the 8B base model.\nThe predictors operate independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.\n\n\n\\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}\n\\label{sec:method:gumbel}\n\nThe hard mask $m_j = \\mathbf{1}[s_j > \\text{threshold}]$ is non-differentiable, preventing gradient flow from the KL objective through the mask to the predictor parameters.\nWe apply Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} to produce differentiable soft masks:\n\\begin{align}\n\\tilde{m}_j = \\sigma_\\tau\\!\\left(\\log \\frac{s_j}{1 - s_j} + g_1 - g_2\\right), \\quad g_1, g_2 \\sim \\text{Gumbel}(0,1),\n\\label{eq:gumbel}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\sigma_\\tau(x) = \\text{sigmoid}(x / \\tau)$ and $\\tau$ is a temperature parameter.\nThe Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\\tau \\to 0$, $\\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.\n\nWe anneal $\\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.\nAt high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons for broad exploration.\nAs temperature decreases, the mask concentrates on confident decisions, approximating the hard allocation used at inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison with Straight-Through Estimation.}\nThe straight-through estimator (STE)~\\cite{bengio2013ste} applies a hard threshold in the forward pass and passes gradients through unchanged ($\\partial m / \\partial s = 1$).\nIn our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously, causing violent oscillations in the penalty weight and failure to converge to the target sparsity.\nGumbel relaxation eliminates this pathology, contributing a 34.5\\% improvement (PPL 15.77 $\\to$ 11.79 at 50\\% sparsity), as we verify empirically in \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}.\n\n\n\\subsection{KL Divergence Training Objective}\n\\label{sec:method:kl}\n\nLet $\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{dense}}$ denote the output logits from a full dense forward pass, and $\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}$ the logits from a forward pass with predictor-generated soft masks $\\{\\tilde{\\mathbf{m}}^{(\\ell)}\\}_\\ell$ applied at every layer.\nWe minimize the forward KL divergence between the dense and sparse output distributions:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} = D_{\\text{KL}}(p_{\\text{dense}} \\,\\|\\, p_{\\text{sparse}}) = \\sum_{v} p_{\\text{dense}}^{(v)} \\log \\frac{p_{\\text{dense}}^{(v)}}{p_{\\text{sparse}}^{(v)}},\n\\label{eq:kl_loss}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $p_{\\text{dense}} = \\text{softmax}(\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{dense}})$ and $p_{\\text{sparse}} = \\text{softmax}(\\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}})$, summed over the vocabulary $v$ and averaged over sequence positions.\nThe base model is frozen; only the predictor parameters receive gradients.\n\nWe use forward KL (mean-seeking) rather than reverse KL, as the goal is to preserve the dense model's primary predictions rather than explore alternative modes; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} validates this choice with ablations over divergence direction (forward KL outperforms JSD by 6.4\\% and reverse KL by 8.6\\%).\nNo temperature scaling ($T{=}1$) is applied~\\cite{hinton2015distilling}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Adaptive Penalty Sparsity Controller}\n\\label{sec:method:lagrangian}\n\nWe enforce a model-level sparsity target $\\rho$ via constrained optimization.\nLet $\\bar{s} = \\frac{1}{L} \\sum_\\ell \\frac{1}{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\sum_j \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}$ denote the average fraction of retained neurons.\nThe constrained problem is:\n\\begin{align}\n\\min_\\theta \\; \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\bar{s} \\leq 1 - \\rho.\n\\label{eq:constrained}\n\\end{align}\nWe relax this via an adaptive penalty controller:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}} + \\lambda \\cdot \\text{clamp}(|\\bar{s} - (1 - \\rho)| - \\epsilon, \\min{=}0)^2,\n\\label{eq:lagrangian}\n\\end{align}\nwhere $\\epsilon = 0.02$ is a deadzone margin that prevents $\\lambda$ from responding to small constraint violations.\nThe penalty weight $\\lambda$ is updated multiplicatively at each step:\n\\begin{align}\n\\lambda \\leftarrow \\begin{cases} \\lambda \\times 2 & \\text{if } \\bar{s} > (1-\\rho) + \\epsilon \\\\ \\lambda \\times 0.5 & \\text{if } \\bar{s} < (1-\\rho) - \\epsilon \\\\ \\lambda & \\text{otherwise} \\end{cases}\n\\label{eq:dual_update}\n\\end{align}\nwith $\\lambda$ clamped to $[\\lambda_{\\text{floor}}, \\lambda_{\\max}]$ where $\\lambda_{\\text{floor}} = 0.01$ and $\\lambda_{\\max} \\in \\{1000, 5000\\}$ depending on the target.\nThe geometric doubling requires only $\\lambda_{\\max}$ as a user-specified hyperparameter (no step-size tuning) and reaches target sparsity within 40--110 steps for 40--50\\% targets; for 30\\% and 70\\% targets, $\\lambda$ saturates at $\\lambda_{\\max}$ with 1.7--5.0\\% residual deviation (Appendix~\\ref{app:lagrangian}, Figure~\\ref{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}). The deadzone prevents oscillation once sparsity is within $\\pm$2\\%.\\footnote{Our update rule resembles the augmented Lagrangian method but replaces gradient-based dual updates with a multiplicative schedule for training stability. We term it an adaptive penalty controller to distinguish from formal dual methods that guarantee KKT satisfaction.}\n\n\n\\subsection{Gradient-Based Motivation: BCE vs.\\ KL}\n\\label{sec:method:gradient}\n\nWe analyze the structural difference between BCE and KL training signals to motivate why KL distillation is better suited to global top-$k$ inference.\n\n\\paragraph{BCE gradient structure.}\nThe standard approach trains the predictor against oracle masks $m_j^* = \\mathbf{1}[|g_j| \\geq \\text{top-}k\\text{ threshold}]$ derived from the dense model's activation magnitudes, where the threshold is the global top-$k$ cutoff across all layers (matching TEAL's inference protocol).\nThe loss decomposes per neuron:\n\\begin{align}\n\\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} = -\\frac{1}{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\sum_{j=1}^{d_{\\text{ff}}} \\bigl[ m_j^* \\log \\sigma(s_j) + (1 - m_j^*) \\log(1 - \\sigma(s_j)) \\bigr].\n\\label{eq:bce}\n\\end{align}\nThe gradient with respect to predictor logit $s_j$ is:\n\\begin{align}\n\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}}}{\\partial s_j} = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*.\n\\label{eq:bce_grad}\n\\end{align}\nThis gradient depends \\emph{only} on neuron $j$'s own prediction and target.\nEach neuron is trained independently; there is no inter-neuron coupling in the learning signal.\n\n\\paragraph{KL gradient structure.}\nUnder KL distillation, the gradient for predictor logit $s_j^{(\\ell)}$ in layer $\\ell$ is:\n\\begin{align}\n\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}}}{\\partial s_j^{(\\ell)}} = \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{KL}}}{\\partial \\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}}}_{\\text{(a)}} \\cdot \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\mathbf{z}_{\\text{sparse}}}{\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}}}_{\\text{(b)}} \\cdot \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)}}{\\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}}}_{\\text{(c)}} \\cdot \\underbrace{\\frac{\\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)}}{\\partial s_j^{(\\ell)}}}_{\\text{(d)}},\n\\label{eq:kl_grad}\n\\end{align}\nwhere each factor has a distinct role:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item[\\textbf{(a)}] The KL gradient at the output, reflecting how each logit component deviates from the dense distribution.\n\\item[\\textbf{(b)}] The Jacobian through all downstream layers from layer $\\ell$'s output to the final logits. This factor couples neuron $j$'s gradient to the state of every other retained neuron in layers $\\ell, \\ell{+}1, \\ldots, L$, because the sparse hidden state propagates through subsequent attention and FFN operations.\n\\item[\\textbf{(c)}] The local mask effect: $\\partial \\mathbf{h}^{(\\ell)} / \\partial \\tilde{m}_j^{(\\ell)} = (g_j^{(\\ell)} \\cdot [\\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}]_j) \\cdot \\mathbf{W}_{d,j}^{(\\ell)}$, projecting through the down-projection to affect the full $d$-dimensional hidden state.\n\\item[\\textbf{(d)}] The Gumbel-Sigmoid derivative, providing the differentiable path from logit to mask.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{The structural mismatch.}\nThe key observation is that factor (b) makes the KL gradient for neuron $j$ depend on \\emph{all} other neurons' mask values.\nBCE's factorized gradient (Eq.~\\ref{eq:bce_grad}) optimizes each neuron's prediction accuracy in isolation, but TEAL inference selects neurons via a global ranking across all layers.\nUnder global top-$k$, the decision to retain neuron $j$ depends on the scores of all other neurons, a combinatorial interaction that BCE cannot capture.\n\nConcretely, BCE achieves high per-neuron accuracy ($>$90\\% oracle agreement) yet produces poor output under TEAL, because it optimizes a proxy misaligned with output preservation under global budget allocation.\nKL directly optimizes the end-to-end objective, with gradients that reflect inter-neuron dependencies through the computation graph.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical verification.}\nMeasuring gradient SNR\\footnote{$\\text{SNR}_\\ell = \\|\\mathbb{E}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]\\|_2^2 / \\text{Var}[\\nabla_{\\theta_\\ell} \\mathcal{L}]$, expectation over ${\\sim}10^{-8}$ after step~10, consistent with rapid convergence of per-neuron classification, though an alternative explanation is loss saturation (gradient vanishing once accuracy plateaus).\nThis preliminary measurement persists across learning rates; full-trajectory characterization is left to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}\n\\label{sec:method:taxonomy}\n\nWe organize prior work along two axes that capture the essential design decisions for sparsity prediction: \\emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \\emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies). Intermediate (MSE) & Coupled (KL) \\\\\n\\midrule\nStatic & --- & --- & SPON \\\\\nPer-token & DejaVu, FastForward & ShadowLLM, D2DMoE & \\textbf{Ours} {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "21dedd1e-b982-4832-91b9-02dab444d41f",
  "uuid": "c1ff3b7e-2e70-42c6-87fb-db170a16c4d6",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "TEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude, achieving 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.",
    "newString": "TEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude, achieving 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision, providing intermediate coupling within each layer but no end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} also employs learnable masks with Gumbel relaxation and frozen weights, but targets N:M semi-structured \\emph{weight} sparsity (2:4 patterns) via per-group categorical selection (Gumbel-Softmax), producing fixed masks independent of input tokens.\nBoth share the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but solve distinct optimization problems: MaskLLM finds optimal static N:M patterns within fixed groups, while we predict dynamic per-token masks under a global budget constraint.\nThe structural mismatch we characterize (per-neuron gradient decomposition vs.\\ global top-$k$) does not arise in MaskLLM's per-group categorical setting, where N:M patterns eliminate cross-group budget competition.\nAll prior predictor-based methods use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nOur contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers, producing input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda} and SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt} produce static weight masks; Switch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Related Work}",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}",
          "-TEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude, achieving 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.",
          "+TEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.",
          " CATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.",
          " All training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  }
}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/appendix.tex",

    "originalFile": "\\appendix\n\n\\section{Inference Efficiency}\n\\label{app:latency}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:latency} reports inference throughput on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (batch size 1, sequence length 2048, fp16, averaged over 100 forward passes).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Inference latency on LLaMA-3.1-8B (RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell, batch=1, seq=2048, fp16, 100 forward passes). Overhead is relative to dense inference.}\n\\label{tab:latency}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Sparsity & ms/fwd & tok/s & Overhead \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 0\\% & 119.11$\\pm$0.57 & 17{,}195 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nKL predictor-only & 30\\% & 126.64$\\pm$0.70 & 16{,}172 & +6.3\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\nKL predictor-only & 50\\% & 126.78$\\pm$0.31 & 16{,}154 & +6.4\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\n\\midrule\nPredictor + compensation & 30\\% & 129.94$\\pm$0.75 & 15{,}761 & +9.1\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\nPredictor + compensation & 50\\% & 129.71$\\pm$0.53 & 15{,}789 & +8.9\\% \\\\ % exp_id: latency_bench\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe full KL-sparse pipeline adds 6.4\\% overhead to dense inference (126.78 vs.\\ 119.11\\,ms/forward), of which the predictor's 32-layer forward pass contributes 3.17\\,ms (2.7\\%); the remainder arises from mask application and sparse FFN indexing.\nEliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\\sim$2.7\\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling), confirming that KL distillation provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.\nThe overhead is near-identical at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity because the predictor runs the same computation regardless of target sparsity; only the top-$k$ threshold changes.\n\nNote that the current implementation uses unstructured element-wise masks, which do not translate to wall-clock speedup without sparse kernel support.\nThe pipeline adds $\\sim$6.4\\% overhead; net acceleration requires kernel-level sparse computation optimizations (e.g., Triton block-sparse, cuSPARSE) that are beyond the scope of this work.\nOur contribution is prediction quality and inference simplification (removing the compensation network), not end-to-end latency reduction.\n\n\n\\section{Predictor Bottleneck Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:db_sensitivity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:db_sensitivity} reports the effect of predictor bottleneck dimension $d_b$ on PPL at 50\\% sparsity under both TEAL global allocation (the paper's primary inference protocol) and per-token evaluation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Predictor bottleneck sensitivity at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). TEAL global is the primary inference protocol; per-token shown for reference.}\n\\label{tab:db_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$d_b$ & Params & TEAL global & $\\Delta$ & Per-token \\\\\n\\midrule\n64 & 38.2M (0.48\\%) & 12.076 & +0.9\\% & 10.16 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n128 & 75.9M (0.95\\%) & 11.963 & --- & 9.218 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n256 & 151.5M (1.89\\%) & 12.105 & +1.2\\% & 8.586 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n512 & 302.5M (3.77\\%) & 12.315 & +2.9\\% & 7.93 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder TEAL global allocation, increasing $d_b$ beyond 128 provides no benefit (PPL slightly worsens), while per-token evaluation shows monotonic improvement (10.16$\\to$7.93).\nThis divergence indicates that the global top-$k$ allocation mechanism, not predictor capacity, is the binding constraint: additional predictor capacity produces better per-neuron scores, but the global ranking and budget distribution cannot exploit them.\nThe $d_b{=}128$ configuration (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base model) is therefore optimal for the TEAL inference protocol.\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Schedule Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:tau_sensitivity}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temperature schedule sensitivity at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, TEAL global).}\n\\label{tab:tau_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau$ Schedule & PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFixed $\\tau{=}0.5$ & \\textbf{11.861} \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\nLinear $1.0 \\to 0.1$ (default) & 11.963 \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\nFixed $\\tau{=}0.1$ & 12.072 \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTemperature schedule has minimal impact ($<$1.8\\% PPL variation).\nThe linear annealing default is a conservative choice; even removing annealing entirely (fixed $\\tau{=}0.5$) yields comparable performance.\n\n\\subsection{Training Convergence}\n\\label{app:training_convergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training convergence at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, TEAL global).}\n\\label{tab:training_convergence}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{cc}\n\\toprule\nSteps & PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n200 & 15.158 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n400 & 12.538 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n600 & 12.046 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n800 & 11.907 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n1000 & \\textbf{11.906} \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTraining converges rapidly: 95\\% of the quality gap closes by step 600, and performance saturates by step 800 ($\\Delta < 0.001$ between 800 and 1000 steps).\nOur default of 1000 steps provides a conservative margin.\n\n\n\\section{Adaptive Penalty Controller Dynamics}\n\\label{app:lagrangian}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the adaptive penalty controller across sparsity targets.\nThe geometric doubling pattern ($1 \\to 2 \\to 4 \\to 8 \\to \\ldots$) enables $\\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\\pm$2\\% of the target.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets (multiplicative schedule, margin $\\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}\n\\label{tab:lagrangian}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nTarget $\\rho$ & $\\lambda_{\\max}$ & Final $\\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42\n40\\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42\n50\\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42\n70\\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.\nAt 30\\% and 70\\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\\lambda$ to its respective caps.\nThe 40\\% target converges to $\\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\\% deviation, illustrating the controller's precision when the target is reachable without saturating.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Adaptive penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets. Upper panel: penalty weight $\\lambda$ (log scale) over training steps. The 30\\% target requires extreme penalty ($\\lambda \\to 5000$) to suppress activations below their natural density, while 50\\% needs only $\\lambda \\approx 8$ since SwiGLU gates already cluster near this sparsity. Lower panel: actual sparsity (solid) converges to the target (dashed) within $\\sim$10 steps via geometric doubling.}\n\\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTemperature $\\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.\nA mild loss increase in the final $\\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\\tau \\to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, confirming the training is converging toward the hard-mask inference regime.\n\nThe controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.\n\n\n\\section{Out-of-Distribution Evaluation}\n\\label{app:ood}\n\nTo address concerns about data homogeneity between training (WikiText-103) and evaluation (WikiText-2), we evaluate on C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4} validation set at 50\\% sparsity under TEAL global allocation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Out-of-distribution evaluation on C4 validation (50\\% sparsity, PPL$\\downarrow$).}\n\\label{tab:ood}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & C4 PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 9.21 \\\\\nKL predictor (Ours) & \\textbf{18.40} \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c4_ood_kl50\nTEAL vanilla & 38.75 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL-trained predictor achieves 52.5\\% relative advantage over TEAL on out-of-domain data (18.40 vs.\\ 38.75), comparable to or slightly exceeding the 49\\% advantage observed on WikiText-2 (11.79 vs.\\ 23.31), suggesting that KL-trained predictions generalize to held-out data when the evaluation distribution differs from training.\nThis confirms that the predictor learns generalizable neuron importance patterns rather than overfitting to the WikiText training distribution.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Retention Analysis}\n\\label{app:retention}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{per_layer_retention_chart.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer neuron retention under global top-$k$ allocation at 30\\%, 50\\%, and 70\\% sparsity. Dashed lines indicate uniform retention baselines. Late layers (L24--31) are consistently starved due to long-tailed activation distributions, with retention dropping to 28.4\\% (L31) even at 30\\% sparsity. At 70\\%, starvation extends to mid-layers (L10--12: 16.7--20.1\\%), explaining the transition to the capacity-limited regime.}\n\\label{fig:retention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Training Stability}\n\\label{app:stability}\n\nThree-seed evaluation ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$) at 50\\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \\pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\\%), confirming training stability across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\nDownstream metrics are similarly stable: ARC-c $33.8 \\pm 1.2$\\%, WinoGrande $62.9 \\pm 0.1$\\%, MMLU $28.0 \\pm 0.6$\\%, GSM8K $2.2 \\pm 0.1$\\%.\n\nUnder global allocation at 50\\%, MMLU is equally stable: $28.1 \\pm 0.2$\\% (s42=27.89, s123=28.34, s456=27.94), confirming that the near-random performance is capacity-limited rather than seed-sensitive ($\\sigma$=0.2pp vs.\\ 9.5pp at 30\\% global). % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\nThe low standard deviation across both allocation strategies indicates that the KL training landscape is well-conditioned for this predictor architecture, with low sensitivity to initialization.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Downstream Evaluation}\n\\label{app:downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:downstream} compares Dense, KL 30\\% (uniform allocation), and TEAL 30\\% on three additional downstream benchmarks not included in the main results.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Extended downstream benchmarks at 30\\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, seed 42). HellaSwag: 10-shot normalized acc; PIQA: 0-shot normalized acc; BoolQ: 0-shot acc.}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & HellaSwag & PIQA & BoolQ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & \\textbf{82.33} & \\textbf{80.74} & \\textbf{82.94} \\\\\nKL 30\\% uniform & 72.70 & 75.95 & 76.85 \\\\ % exp_id: downstream_extended_kl30_s42\nTEAL 30\\% vanilla & 66.70 & 73.61 & 68.50 \\\\ % exp_id: downstream_teal30_baseline\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL predictor outperforms TEAL on all three benchmarks. On HellaSwag, it preserves 88.3\\% of dense accuracy (72.70 vs.\\ 82.33) vs.\\ 81.0\\% for TEAL (66.70). BoolQ shows the largest gap: 92.7\\% vs.\\ 82.6\\% preservation. the deadzone ($\\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\\pm$2\\% of the target.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets (multiplicative schedule, margin $\\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}\n\\label{tab:lagrangian}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nTarget $\\rho$ & $\\lambda_{\\max}$ & Final $\\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42\n40\\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42\n50\\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42\n70\\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.\nAt 30\\% and 70\\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\\lambda$ to its respective caps.\nThe 40\\% target converges to $\\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\\% deviation, illustrating the controller's precision when the target is reachable without saturating.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Adaptive penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets. Upper panel: penalty weight $\\lambda$ (log scale) over training steps. The 30\\% target requires extreme penalty ($\\lambda \\to 5000$) to suppress activations below their natural density, while 50\\% needs only $\\lambda \\approx 8$ since SwiGLU gates already cluster near this sparsity. Lower panel: actual sparsity (solid) converges to the target (dashed) within $\\sim$10 steps via geometric doubling.}\n\\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTemperature $\\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.\nA mild loss increase in the final $\\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\\tau \\to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, confirming the training is converging toward the hard-mask inference regime.\n\nThe controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.\n\n\n\\section{Out-of-Distribution Evaluation}\n\\label{app:ood}\n\nTo address concerns about data homogeneity between training (WikiText-103) and evaluation (WikiText-2), we evaluate on C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4} validation set at 50\\% sparsity under TEAL global allocation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Out-of-distribution evaluation on C4 validation (50\\% sparsity, PPL$\\downarrow$).}\n\\label{tab:ood}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & C4 PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 9.21 \\\\\nKL predictor (Ours) & \\textbf{18.40} \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c4_ood_kl50\nTEAL vanilla & 38.75 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL-trained predictor achieves 52.5\\% relative advantage over TEAL on out-of-domain data (18.40 vs.\\ 38.75), comparable to or slightly exceeding the 49\\% advantage observed on WikiText-2 (11.79 vs.\\ 23.31), suggesting that KL-trained predictions generalize to held-out data when the evaluation distribution differs from training.\nThis confirms that the predictor learns generalizable neuron importance patterns rather than overfitting to the WikiText training distribution.\n\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Retention Analysis}\n\\label{app:retention}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{per_layer_retention_chart.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer neuron retention under global top-$k$ allocation at 30\\%, 50\\%, and 70\\% sparsity. Dashed lines indicate uniform retention baselines. Late layers (L24--31) are consistently starved due to long-tailed activation distributions, with retention dropping to 28.4\\% (L31) even at 30\\% sparsity. At 70\\%, starvation extends to mid-layers (L10--12: 16.7--20.1\\%), explaining the transition to the capacity-limited regime.}\n\\label{fig:retention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Training Stability}\n\\label{app:stability}\n\nThree-seed evaluation ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$) at 50\\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \\pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\\%), confirming training stability across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\nDownstream metrics are similarly stable: ARC-c $33.8 \\pm 1.2$\\%, WinoGrande $62.9 \\pm 0.1$\\%, MMLU $28.0 \\pm 0.6$\\%, GSM8K $2.2 \\pm 0.1$\\%.\n\nUnder global allocation at 50\\%, MMLU is equally stable: $28.1 \\pm 0.2$\\% (s42=27.89, s123=28.34, s456=27.94), confirming that the near-random performance is capacity-limited rather than seed-sensitive ($\\sigma$=0.2pp vs.\\ 9.5pp at 30\\% global). % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\nThe low standard deviation across both allocation strategies indicates that the KL training landscape is well-conditioned for this predictor architecture, with low sensitivity to initialization.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Downstream Evaluation}\n\\label{app:downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:downstream} compares Dense, KL 30\\% (uniform allocation), and TEAL 30\\% on three additional downstream benchmarks not included in the main results.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Extended downstream benchmarks at 30\\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, seed 42). HellaSwag: 10-shot normalized acc; PIQA: 0-shot normalized acc; BoolQ: 0-shot acc.}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & HellaSwag & PIQA & BoolQ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & \\textbf{82.33} & \\textbf{80.74} & \\textbf{82.94} \\\\\nKL 30\\% uniform & 72.70 & 75.95 & 76.85 \\\\ % exp_id: downstream_extended_kl30_s42\nTEAL 30\\% vanilla & 66.70 & 73.61 & 68.50 \\\\ % exp_id: downstream_teal30_baseline\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL predictor outperforms TEAL on all three benchmarks. On HellaSwag, it preserves 88.3\\% of dense accuracy (72.70 vs.\\ 82.33) vs.\\ 81.0\\% for TEAL (66.70). BoolQ shows the largest gap: 92.7\\% vs.\\ 82.6\\% preservation. PIQA shows a smaller differential (94.1\\% vs.\\ 91.2\\%).\n",
